A GRATEFUL MAN.

(Copyright.)

===== 0002601_19040604_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
NURSE MA.!%A_RJORIE.

By SHIRLEY RAYNARD,

duthor of "The Ghost of the West Wing," "The Turn of the Tide," "Madge of the Manse," "Robert Lanyon's Mistake," "A Wife's Temptation," &c.

London on Bank Holiday is not particula4y cheerful at the best of times. Numerous trains, from all the principal stations, carry off their loads of holiday-makers to the country far and near, leaving the streets almost deserted. It was August, and the multitudes were revelling in a glorious sunshine. It fell, too, upon the deserted streets, making the footpaths hot and tiring to walk upon, and shewing up grime and dust, which would pass unobserved in the traffic of an ordinary day. Few 'buses were running, and the greater number of cabbies seemed to have joined the general exodus to the country. A gentleman walked leisurely down Cheapside, looking round from time to time in search of a passing hansom. He was a tall and well-built man, and, if not exactly handsome, in the strict sense of the word, had passable features, and a certain air which impressed people even at the first glance. He was dressed with a somewhat careless ease. There was nothing of the Bond-street spick-and-span smartness about him, though his coat fitted to

perfection -and his linen was spotless. He was a man who became fond of an old coat, and could with difficulty persuade himself to throw it aside. His step was firm but not aggressive, and as he walked he was so erect that he appeared taller than his actual inches. His age was thirty, hot he scarcely seemed, so old. His face was bronzed, and it could plainly be seen that he had spent much time in the open air. At the Bank he was fortunate in finding a 'bus going in the right direction. He stepped up on the outside, glad to be relieved from walking further in the hot sun. He was hardly seated when a man crossed over from the other side of the 'bus to the seat behind him, which happened to be vacant. "How are you, Cunningham ? " said he, shaking him by the hand. "I had no idea you were at -home again. Staying in town long?" "A few days only," said Ainsley Cunningham, turning to face his interlocutor. landed last evening, and have put up at the Langham. I have a few business matters to look to before going North. Have you been long in town?" questioned he. "A few weeks; up on business as usual," returned Sidney Chance in his most indifferent voice,avoiding the other's direct gaze. "I am often in town nowadays. I hope you are quite strong now, after your long travels, he went on, by way of changing the subject. "Never was better," said Cunningham, smiling. "I could have come home a year ago, but I was determined, if possible, to make a perfect cure of myself this time.. 'Now they tell me that, with ordinary cege, I may live to see a hundred." They c.isor'Lted on for a time, and presently the 'bus stopped in front of a hospital, and Cunning- ham, saying good-bye to his companion, stepped down. Chance wa's rising at the same time, but sat down again, slightly confused, and went on with the 'bus. Cunningham noticed his son- fusion' and wondered for a moment what had caused it; but he soon dismissed the man and his affairs from his mind. Chance was a man whom Cunningham had known from his college days. He had run across him from time to time since leaving Oxford, but he had never sought his society. He always felt an inward distrust of him, although he had no grounds for the feeling. He was a doctor by profession, and had worked hard, earning for himself a fair position. He spent his winters in the South of France, making a good deal of money by his season's practice, and having—which suited him well—a good deal of leisure during the summer months. Cunningham passed the porter's lodge, and went into the hospital. He obtained an order to see "Nurse Marjorie," and after threading many passages, and losing his way once or twice, he at last found her, just leaving the nurse's sitting- room. -"0 Ainsley!" she exclaimed, as she came suddenly upon him. "Who would have expected to see you here ?" "Did you not get my letter ?" said he, turning into the room with her. "I wrote telling you I would call." "I have not had my letters this morning," said she; "they are probably waiting for me in the Nursing Home. I left early, and the post was not in. How long have you been home?" "Only landed last night," said he. "I found a pile of letters waiting for me at my hotel, and amongst them one from my mother, in which she tells me of your having taken up nursing in this hospital. Of course, I came as soon as rovible to look you up." "It is extremely kind of you," said she, looking out of the window at the dirty brick wall opposite. "You are more ceremonious with me, Marjorie, +than you used to be in the old days," said he, with a touch of sadness in his tone. "Am I? " said she, laughing. "You forget that l'have been three months in London, and I have spent a good deal of that time in learning to put .my original self into the background." -"Then for goodness' sake leave the hospital and come home, and be the old Marjorie again." "I am afraid that is impossible," said she, with :a sigh. Her hands lay idly in her lap, and there was a listlessness about her which seemed to Cunningham foreign to her nature. He hs. l never noticed it in the old days. whatever it might be, ,and it troubled him. He looked at her as she sat, the light falling upon her clear pale face, and warming the brown of her soft wavy hair, which she wore drawn loosely back from her temples. Her gown was of the regulation lilac-cotton, but was made to tit perfectly her rounded, slender figure. All about her person bespoke the daintiest cleanliness and refinement. It was a wonderfully sweet face that. he looked upon, and none of its charm was lost upon him. How often, when thousands of miles from home, in the long and dreary nights, had he tried to conjure up its features and bestow upon them their sunny smile. Marjorie Howard was the only daughter of the Vicar of Ashbrook. a lovely parish in North York- shire. She and Ainsley Cunningham had played together as children. Though he was five years her senior, they were always good friends. The little girl of eight years would run after her big boy friend, and beg to be taken upon the various fishing expeditions, .&c.., upon which he might be engaged.

Most times, she had her way, for the boy found it hard to resist the pleading of the soft brown eyes. Cunningham Hall was situated within half-a-mile of Ashbrook, and the intercourse was frequent between it and the Vicarage. especially as, for some years, Mr. Howard acted as the boy's tutor. At sixteen, Marjorie was sent abroad to school, where she remained three years, returning home a woman in mind and physique, and within a very short time Ainsley Cunningham discovered that his old boyish affection for the girl had changed into a deep an passionate love. It was just about this time, however, that he began to develop a decided weakness of the lungs, and knowing this, he did his utmost to hide his love from the girl he worshipped. Finally, after two years' indifferent health, be was sent abroad, where a slow but what now seemed a perfect cure had been effected. His love for Tklarjerie had burnt clear and true through the years of separation, and now, upon his return, he felt free to gain her love, if possible. _ _

"I for-got, Marjorie," he said at length, "that your time is not your own. I hope I have not kept you too long. When are you free to come out of this dreadful piece for a few hours ?" "It is not so dreadful when you get used to it," said she, smiling. "But you have not kept me too long. I had two hours off this morning. Saturday I am free the whole day, but where could we go? It is not at all proper,. you know, for us•to go walking about without a chaperon," she said, with a merry twinkle in her eyes. "Oh, never mind about the chaperon ; you did not trouble about her in the old days, " said he, lau!hing. "We will go a-fishing, if you like," he added, playfully, "though 1 don't see much chance in this neighbourhood."

_ After arranginr, •to call for her on Saturday morning, he took' his leare, and walked back through the dreary stone passages to the entrance. Just as he descended the steps, he caught sight of Sidney Chance crossing the open space towards the door. He prised him by with a nod, and made his way back through the deserted streets to his hotel.

Chance entered the hotpital and went into a room on the ground-floor, He had •several acquaintances upon whom he was in the habit of calling, for he had no professional connection with the hospital. He might often be seen in the corridors, but his appearance there caused no

remark. He was in love with Nurse Marjorie, whom he had met first in Yorkshire, and after- wards at the house of a mutual friend in town. He was leavinff no stone unturned to make her his own. He would waylay her on her walks, ana accompany her back to the hospital. He would meet her in the passages as she ran from ward to ward during her day work. He seemed to have a wonderful means of discovering her whereabouts. She seldom spoke to him, except when off duty, and then never willingly. She had done all that she could think of to repulse him without absolute rudeness, but it seemed to have little or no effect. Latterly she had become afraid of him, for though she disliked and distrusted him, he seemed to have some power over her which she could not under- stand. He had immense will-power and animal magnetism, and he made use of them in his conversations with her. He was unscrupulous in the means he used so long as he could get her into his power. He quite realised her dislike to himself, but he meant to possess her in spite of that. There was little of the spiritual element in his passion; it was of the earth, earthy. Marjorie was passing down the stairs some half- hour after her visitor had left, when upon turning one of the corners she came upon Mr. Chance. "Good-morning, Miss -Howard," he said, blandly. "I hear you have had a visit from our old friend Cunningham." "Yes," answered she, coolly returning his greet- ing. "You must excuse me," she added, as he tried to detain her, "I am busy, and you know how strict our rules are." _

"Did you•explain this to Cunningham ? " sneered

"Mr. Cunningham had an order to see me, and I was off duty at the time. The cases are not the same. lam due in my ward in a moment. Please let me pass." "Just a minute," said he, more softly, detaining her by his curious gaze. "When is your free day?" "Saturday," she said, simply, but. in a moment regretted having told him, for she felt be would make some use of the inforniation. He stood aside and she passed down the stairs to her ward, but she could not forget for hours the unpleasant effect of the interview.

_ True to his promise Cunningham appeared on Saturday morning, and in defiance of convention took his old playmate out for a long day in the country. The heat had somewhat abated since the Monday, and there had been a day or two of refreshing rain. The Surrey lanes were very sweat, and the girl seemed to draw in fresh life from their fragrance. It was a long day of calm happiness for them both. Cunningham reminded her of all their merry childish jaunts. He was careful of her slightest wish, but. he never uttered a word of his great love, fearing to drive her from him by speaking in too great haste. Though he had loved Marjorie for years, he had no reason for thinking that she regarded him in any other light than that of friendship. The evening drew in all too soon and brought them back to town. They drove from the station to the hospital gates, and then Ainsley helped the girl out of the hansom and took her hand. "Good-bye, Marjorie," he said, with warmth. "I shall be back in town before many weeks are over. Promise me that you will spend another day along with me then." "I may not have a day off," said she, mischievously. , "Then I will sta,Y.in town until you have one," he answered. Sha turned towarhS the gate, and he sprang into the hansom. She paused a moment watching it spin swiftly away, and then, as she was about to enter the gateway, she came face to face with Chance. He was about the last person she would have chosen to see at that minute. He made a bad finish to an otherwise perfect day. "Your time is. not yet up," he said, coolly, drawing out his watch. "You have spent a day with that fellow Cunningham, and now surely you can talk to me for a few minutes." His accents. were choked with passion. "You forget yourself, Mr. Chance," said Majorie, proudly stepping back as she felt his strange eyes upon her. "No," he said. "I very much remember myself, and I mean that you shall remember me too. You have avoided me long enough, Miss Howard. Marjorie," he said, taking her hand alicnost roughly and holding it in his iron clasp, "do you not know that I love you to distraction, that my life is worth nothing without you ? I must, and will have you, Marjorie. I swear it by all the powers!" Marjorie struggled-to speak and to withdraw her hand, but she was powerless for the moment. "Promise me that you will never, never marry Cunningham," he burst forth, passionately, "or I. will never let you go." "I have no intention of marrying him," she said. "He does not want me," she added, trying to calm him, but in:Tier heart only half .believing the words that came from her lips. "Will you promise to marry me, then, and shew that you mean what you say ? " "Oh, indeed, I cannot promise," said she, beginning to tremble. "You know quite well that I have no love for you. I have tried to shew you in every way that it is quite, quite useless." "Never mind the love," he said, bitterly. "I have enough for us both. .Willyou marry me 1" _ _ _ _

"Oh, please let me go," said she; "the clock is striking and I shall be late." "Well," he said, "I shall not take your answer. You must think of me, Marjorie, for I cannot live without you, and I mean to marry you whether you will, or no!"

Our best intention § are often thwarted by some small incident, which, happening at another time, wcaild seem a -trifle. Early in September, a few days before Cunningham proposed to return to town, he was out shooting, when in climbing a stile he slipped and sprained his ankle rather badly. He suffered some amount of pain from it, but this was nothing compared to the mental anguish that possessed him when he found that it would delay for some weeks his visit to Marjorie. He wrote to her telling her the cans) of his absence, and received a short note of carefully-expressed sympathy in return. There was a postscript at the bottom of her note. It, ran: "I have a week's holiday at the beginning of October. - I am going to spend it with the Grayhams, of Dorking." Now, the Grayhams were old friends of the Cunninghams, as well as of the Howards. They had, since their recent removal South, asked Ainsley to visit them upon the first oppor- tunity. He had put the letter in his pocket- book with the intention of answering it at his leisure. That leisure he now found, and wrote to his old friends at some length. He spoke of his slight accident, and said that he expected to be laid up for about three weeks. At the end of that time, he said, he should be only too glad to accept their hospitality. He would visit them at the beginning of October, if that would fall in with their arrangements.

The weeks seemed weary waiting to the impatient 'man, lying with his foot supported on a cushion, but they passed away at length. The lame foot gained strength from day to day, until by the end of September he was able to walk short distances. In another three days he was off. His mother, wondering at his haste to be zone, tried to per- suade him that his visit to Dorking would be equally pleasant the following week. "I don't think so, mother," he said, gently, but with decision. So the thiid of October saw him in the nine o'clock express to the South. How delightful it was to be skimming through the sun- lit country, every mile bringing him nearer to the woman he loved! He had resolved to delay no more, but tell her of his love and ask her to be his wife. He reached London early in the afternoon, and about two hours later found himself at Dorking. He received a hearty welcome from his old friends. Both the dear old people rushed out to meet him as the dog-cart drove up to the door. . _

_ "I hope you will itot find us dull, Ainsley," said Mrs. Grayhain, shortly afterwards, settling back into her deep armchair. "Of course he won't.," said her husband, reprovingly; "he is not that kind. Besides, we have a visitor here to help hint to pass the time." "Indeed?" said Cunningham, feeling rather guilty. "Yes," returned the old man, innocently. "We have Marjorie Howard here, resting from that horrible hospital work that she has taken up. I am sure it is enough to kill an ordinary woman. I tell her a woman ought to be made of iron to stand it."

"Where is she now 7" asked Cunningham, trying to sound unconcerned.

- - we sent her out an hour ago. I am deter- mined she shall have all the fresh air she can get whilst she is here. She came lookino, like a ghost, and she is bent upon going back to that grind next week. You might ,ro and mee her when you have had a cup of tea," he added. "She is sure to have gone to the top of .Box Hill, and I will shew you the way she returns. Wo have not told her we expocted you, so you can give her a little surprise. I m mild accompany you, but my breath is not good for_ climbing hills uowadays. But Derbaus your

oot is too tired to walk. lam forgetting you save been an invalid." _. ...

"Not at all. I shall be delighted to go," said Cunningham, hastily. "I have been resting all day and shall enjoy a walk of a mile or two very much."

Half-an-hour later he was making steadily for Box Hill. all impatient for a siqht of Marjorie. At. each turning of the winding, uneven lane lie strained eye and ear to catch sight or sound of her approaching figure. At length, when some distance up the hill, he felt bound to rest, fearing that by walking too great a distance his foot might give out before reaching home. He therefoie climbed the hedge end sat down at the edge of a field, facing a most lovely view of the surrounding country, and possessed himself with what patience he could. As he sat he was hidden from the sight of persons approaching as they descended the lane, though by turning slightly he could see between the bushes. The view was exquisite and typically English, mile after mile of undulating, well-wooded country stretching out before him. The sun, now very low in the heavens, touched the red roofs of the villages, throwing up a brilliant colour against the blue distance. At length lie heard footsteps. and looking round was surprised to see, not only Marjorie Howard but the figure of a man besides. They were in shadow, and for a moment he did not recognise Chance. He was about to spring to his feet to meet them when second thoughts kept him back. Surely Chance could not be there at Marjorie's bidding! No, he felt sure he was obtruding him- self upon the girl. By remaining quiet he would be able to take time for thought and so help Marjorie to better purpose. As they came nearer he saw that Marjorie's face was very pale, but there was a set, determined expression upon her features. He could see by the nervous clasping of her hands that she was undergoing some mental strain. On the other hand, Chance face, though no less determined than Marjorie's, was flushed with excitement. As fortune would have if they paused directly opposite to where Cunningham was seated. The bush was so thick that he could not see them at all clearly now, but he could dis- tinctly hear every word that was spoken. Chance was speaking rapidly. "I will go no further, Miss 'Award—Marjorie," he said, passionately, "until you have promised to become my wife."

"Thet I fear you will have to remain where you are," said she haughtily, taking a step forward. "Not so quickly," said Chance, intercepting her progress. "You will please listen first to what I have to say. I have wooed you all these long month a apparently to no purpose, although I believe that had that vile fellow Cunningham kept off my track, I should have made you care for me. Now I am going to take another plan, and under- stand, please, that you are dealing yith a desperate man, 4ne who holds life cheap. I have come here to-day all prepared for a rapid journey. Now, unless you promise me upon oath that you will marry me within a month's time I will go north and shoot Cunningham. This is no mere idle threat I mean what I say. If you will not marry me, y m shall not have the chance of marrying him. "I cannot -ever marry you," said Marjorie, in deep distress, trembling from head to foot and looking as if she would drop. "You are the greatest coward upon God's earth. How you came to find me in this out-of-the-way place I cannot tell. Oh, what shall I do? " said she at length, bursting into passionate tears. Cunningham could stand no more. Springing at one bound from the hedgerow, it was the work of a moment to grip Chance firmly by the collar and shake him with all his strength, mid finally to fling him into the roadside. "And so this is the way a man honoured and trusted in his profession behaves himself in private life. Coward! Brute! " said Cunningham. "Be- gone, and never let me set eyes on your face againi As for Miss Howard, if you ever so nnich as attempt to speak to her in the future I will expose your whole conduct, not only in this affair, but in another which has recently come to my knowledge. You will remember a little affair of a cheque which you drew in my name some years ago. It would have served two purposes had you carried out the threat I just overheard you make to Miss Howard. Full particulars of that little episode are in the possession of my solicitor, who, in the event qf my sudden death, would make use of them." Chance's first. attitude was one of defiance, but upon fully realising Cunningham's meaning he muttered some indistinct sentences and turned away. The expression upon his face would be difficult to describe. It was a compound of hate and fear. He took his way slowly in the direction from which he had come and was soon lost to siiht. Twilight was falling and all nature was very still. Marjorie, who had leant for support against a gnarled old oak, now came forward. "How can I thank you ? " she said, holding out her hand fO Cunningham. He took the hand she offered, and, placing it upon his arm, b g n to lead her quietly down the rugged lane.

47,Chank God I was near," said he, gravely, and then they walked on in silence for some time.

"Mariorie," he said at length, "are you too upset to listen to me now ? Will you let me protect and care for you always? I have loved you for years so very dearly, and have come here to ask you t.) be my wife. Can you love mr, my darling?" Marjorie's answer must have been satisfactory, for after it had fallen from her lips he paused in the rugged descent and clasped her in his arms. Evening had closed in before they reached Dorking. Dinner had waited some time, and their host and hostess were working themselves into great excitement, thinking their visitors were lost in the wilds of Box Hill. They were, however, reassured when they arrived safe and sound. To this day old Mr. Grayhain claims, laughingly, that he found Cunningham a wife.

Chance has been seen but little—in England since that memorable day. He now spends his summers, as well as his winters, abroad.

EXCITING OTTER HUNT.

I An exciting otter hunt took place at Maryport on i Tuesday. The quarry took to the old mill-race, over ! which a modern portion of the town has been built, and swam into the subterranean passage which carries the race under the shops and houses of three streets. The 1 pack, hot en the scent, followed: and the huntsmen, afraid of the hounds being drowned or suffocated, were relieved when they re-appeared two hundred yards lower down. The otter was left in the passage, probably having escaped into a cellar drain.

LONDON'S OLD RESTAURANT.

Of all London restaurants none has ever been more famous or more characteristic than Simpson's, and it was quite a blow to a clientele of epicures when the Strand improvements necessitated the closure of the old building. Simpson's, moreover, was a place which has always held great attrac tions for the country cousin on his visits te London and was generally accounted one of the sights of the town. So it was with great satisfaction that the announcement that' it was to be rebuilt was received, and last week it once more opened its hospitable portals in the, Strand—a newer and more ornate Simpson's perhaps but still. possessing all the old characteristics. The prospective diner now enters an early Georgian hall with a broad oak staircase, and off it is a large. white- panelled smol;ing-room. On the ground floor is the gentlemen's dining-room, 56 feet by 32 feet, and panelled with oak 'to the height of 2 3 feet. divided into boxes. Upstairs

Is the ladkes' .dining-room ot practically simi- lar dimensions decorated in the Adam style and panelled with white. There are other reception rooms and supplementary dining - rooms, of which one for private parties will hold thirty-five people and is to be the headquarters of the Knights of the Round Table. In it will be found the celebrated mahogany table in one piece. All the other old features have been pre- tierved—the joints on trolleys, each with its larver, the old-fashioned type of English tvaiter, the quality and character of the good ind wine, the saddles of mutton and rounds if beef, the great salmon and hage turbot, the Scotch ale and old claret. followed by tie chessmen and dominoes ! Only one tinoyation has been permitted, and that is b be commended. The kitchens no 'longer ilut at half-past nine sharp, and suppers are row provided for the hungry theatre - goer. simpson's has received a very cordial telcome back to life.

THE WEEK.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COBDEN CENTENARY,

A hundred years ago yesterday Richard Cobden first sag the light. Though a Sussex village has the distinction of being his birthplace, his name is inseparably associated with Lancashire. This week in every corner of Great Britain the centenary"of Cobden's birth is being celebrated by his admirers. The hundredth anniversary comes at a peculiarly appropriate time, and it may almost be described as topical,as after fifty years of almost un- challenged acceptance the chief plank of his policy is now being actively called into question, and the country is taking sides upon it almost as hotly as it did in the thirties and forties. Cobden was, heart and soul, the advocate of Free Trade,which for sá long has been the dominating policy of Great Britain, but now it seems that the next General Election, whether near at hand or distant in date, will be fought on the question of a continuance or a reversal of this fundamental dogma of politics. In addition to Free Trade, peace, economy, and non-intervention were the great principles of his political creed, and his desire was not to see England the leading power in war but in peace, extending trade and exchanging the sword for the shuttle. He was the founder and leader of the Manchester school in politics ; and though a Sussex man by birth, it is with Manchester that Cobden's name will always be principally associated, as it was in connection with that great commercial centre that he evolved his policy, which roused such hostility in the agricultural circles in which he was bred. As a speaker he was always lucid and convincing, and, above all, had the great rhetorical gift of carrying his audience with him sending them along believing when they had come to scoff, persuaded against their will by his masterly eloquence. Another asset in politics was the high respect with which he was re- garded even by his bitterest opponents, who never accused, him of self-seeking, but honoured his single-mindedness and purity of purpose. Money and office had no charm for him, and few men who have fought the keen fight of politics can boast such a conspicuously honourable and untarnished record.

THE FOUNDERS OF OUR FREE TRADE SYSTEM.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
RICHARD COBDEN.

Richard Cobden, the Apostle of Free Trade, was born in 1804 at Heyshott, near Midhurst, in Sussex. He belonged to old yeoman stock. On leaving school he became clerk in his uncle's warehouse in the city of London, and then a commercial traveller in the cotton trade. When he was twenty- four years old he and two friends set up in business on their own account. They quickly prospered, and Cobden was able to devote time to public affairs. He taught himself French, practised English composition, and travelled. In 1839 lie joined the•Anti-Corn Law Association, and was acknowledged leader. In 1841 he was elected M.P. for Stockport. Mr. Morley says that his maiden speech was much more than a success in the ordi- nary sense of attracting the attention of the most difficult of all audiences. They recognised the rare tone of reality and the note of a man dealing with things and not words. His principal work, however, was on the platform. He spent his life in public meetings. He and John Bright went up and down the country arguing, exhorting, and appealing for funds for the crusade. While he was in pursuit of this public work his private business suffered, and at the moment of his triumph he was a ruined man. A public subscription was raised, and £BO,OOO collected. A considerable part was used to discharge his business obligations, and the rest invested. Cobden twice refused Ministerial office, but rendered great service to the nation in the Commercial Treaty with France. He received another subscription for that work. He died in London of bronchitis in his sixty-second year. In the House of Commons Mr. Disraeli paid a very high tribute to his memory.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
JOHN BRIGHT.

The main facts of Jchn Isright's career are familiar to the public. He was, born at Rochdale 1811, the son of a cotton-mill owner, to which business be succeeded, and was a member of the Society of Friends.l Cobden was invited by him in 1837 to speak at an education meeting in Rochdale. Bright was then twenty-six years of age, and greatly impressed the visitor, and a couple of year,: afterwards the two appeared together at an anti-

===== 0002601_19040604_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
BIOGRAPFHOAL NOTES.

corn law meeting at Balton. Bright was reluctant to enter public life, but some' little time after the death of his young wife he was persuaded by Cobden to do so. In the movement which followed the two were held to be the complements and correla- tives of each other. "Cobden," as the late Mr. P. W. Clayden observed, "had the calmness and confidence of the political philosopher, Bright the passion and the fervour of the popular orator. Cobden did the reasoning, Bright supplied the declamation, but mingling argument with appeal." Lord Salisbury paid John Bright the tribute of saying that he was the greatest orator that his generation, or indeed several generations, had seen. He held office twice under Mr. Gladstone as Chan- cellor of the Duchy of Lancaster, but resigned when Alexandria was bombarded. He was also emphatic in his dissent from Mr. Gladstone's Home Rule proposals.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
CHARLES PELHAM VILLIERS.

The Right Hon. Charles Pelham Villiers had several claims to distinction7-not the least being the length of his political. career. He was born ic 1802, was called to the Bar at Lincoln's-inn in 1827, and became one of the members in the House of Commons for Wolverhampton in 1835. He remained M.P. for the same constituency, without a break, for sixty-two years! As an independent Liberal member he was one of the most prominent leaders of the Anti-Corn Law agitation. He began to make his motion in Parliament when the prospect of any success seemed hopeless, and he pursued it with untiring energy and courage. Mr. Cobden fully recognised the part he played in securing the triumph. In 1853 Mr. Villiers joined the Liberal Government as Judge Advocate-General; and later he became President of the Poor-Law Board. While holding that office he revised the poor-law system, by an Act that he carried through Parliament in the face of considerable criticism. In 1879 a marble statue of Mr. Villiers was unveiled by the late Earl Granville at Wolverh,:mpton, when his work tor Free Trade was eulogi-ed. On the Home Rule split Mr. Villiers became a Liberal-Unionist.- In later years he was very little seen in Parliament. But the electors of Wolverhampton were in lulgent, on account of his long and distinguisked association with the town.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
HENRY ASH WORTH.

Henry Ashworth was born at Birtvhstle. near Bolton, in Lancashire, in 1794, and was educated at the famous school of. the Society of Friends at Ackworth. In partnership with a brother ne became proprietor of large cotton-mills. He was one of the founders of the Anti-Corn Law League. and an intimate friend of Cobden. With Bright and Cobden he made a tour of the northern counties to obtain information as to the state of agriculture, and they were known as the "A •13 e" of the League, "gone to ‘study farming." Mr. Ashworth was the mover of the resolution at the great meet- ing in Manchester that £250,000 sterlivg should be raised to prosecute the agitation. lie published a volume of recollections of Cobden. He was a member of the Society of Friends, but with a curious fondness for the use of the gun. At the age of eighty he was still a remarkably good shot.. He died at Florence in his eighty-sixth year, the cause being fever contracted while travelling near Rome.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
GEORGE WILSON.

George Wilson, chairman of the Anti-Corn Law League, was born at Hathersage, Derbyshire, in 1808. His father was a miller there, but taking up a corn merchant's business in Manchester he.put his son to school there. Mr. Wilson was taken into the corn business, but, profiting by instruction from Dr. Dalton, the eminent chemist, he struck out a line for himself in the starch and gum trade, and from that to public work. As a youth he was president of the Manchester. Phrenological Society, and as a young man secretary to the committee which obtained the charter of incorporation for Manchester in 1830. He was made a member of the Executive. Committee of the Anti-Corn Law Association when it was formed in January, 1839, and was chosen chairman in 1841, when the title was turned into that of League. When the League was dissolved, its occupation being gone out with the repeal of the Corn Laws, he was presented with a cheque for £lO,OOO, as a tribute to his splendid service as an organiser. 'The League was revived for a brief period when Lord Derby talked oi re-introducing a moderate duty on corn. In later years Mr. Wilson was president of the National Reform Union. The year before his sudden death. in IE7O he was made chairman of the Lancashire anti Yorkshire Railway Company.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL LEAGUE.

Presentation to Mr. T. P. Bradshaw.

The annual general meeting of the Warrington and District Association Football League was held at the Marquis of Granby, Church-street, on Thursday evening under the presidency of Mr. T. P. Bradshaw (chairman of the league).—The statement of accounts was pre- sented by Mr. W. H. Smalley (hon. treasurer). The year commenced with a balance in hand of £6 Bs. 3d., and after paying expenses there was now a credit balance of £8 4s. 3d.

The CHAIRMAN said the league was in a very good position financially, and the past season was about the best they had had. They were going the right way to have a good reserve fund for future years. The balance sheet was adopted. _

The officers were elected is follows :—President, Mr. William Chalmers; vice-presidents, Messrs. F. J. Whittle (who retired frnm the presidency after two years' service), J. F. D ivey and H. Isherwood; chair- man, Mr. T. P. Bradshaw; secretary, Mr. C. T. Cox; treasurer, Mr. W. H. Smalley; auditors, Messrs. J. A. Banks and J. J. Kite. _

The meeting was then adjourned until July 28th, when the revision of rules' admisEl_on of new clubs, arranging of fixtures, &c., will be considered. A largely attended "smoker" was afterwards held, Mr. F. J. Ivhittle presiding. The newly-elected presi- dent was also present. The toast of The Warrington and District Football League" was proposed by Mr. Harvey, who paid a tribute to the work of the officials. Speaking of Mr. Bradshaw, he said he had done sterling work for the i league. He bad taken a great interest n association football for many years, and he (Mr. Harvey) might safely say that no man in Warrington had done more to further the game than Mr. Bradshaw. With regard to the secretary, Mr. Cox stepped into the position vacated at a very i important time in the history of the league and he had carried out the duties very success- fully. Mr. W. H. SMALLEY responded.

A very interesting event took place in the presenta- tion of a handsome dressing case to Mr. Bradshaw in recognition of his eight years' services as chairman. Mr. WHITTLE made the presentation and eulogised Mr. Bradshaw's work. He did not think there was any man in Warrington who had given more time and more willing service to the furtherance of association foot- ball locally. The gift had been subscribed for not only by members of the league, but by friends of Mr. Brad- shaw who were lovers of football and thought he was deserving of some recognition for the services he had rendered. In handing over the dressing c,ase,Mr.Whittle expressed the hope that the recipient would live for many years to enjoy the use of it. (Applause.) Mr. BRADSHAW said he was rather taken by surprise and hardly knew how to thank them. In the first place he wished to take the opportunity of thanking the mem- bers of the league for re-electing him to the office of Chairman for eight years in succession. He need hardly say that lie had the interests of the game at heart, and he would be willing to do all he could to promote the interests of football in the Warrington district. As Chairman of the league, he claimed that it was conducted on sound business principles, and further that it would compare favourably with any league in the district. He did not for one moment imagine that every decision he had given had met with the approval of the delegates. He did not expect that. He did not, however, think that some of the delegates had taken the decisions of the committee with the grace that they should have done. He was sorry that there had been so many cases of misconduct on the field during the past season. The association had dealt with almost 100 cases,andhe thought the number was too high con- sidering the small radius covered by the league. Foot- ball could be played• and would have to be played in a proper and sportsmarlike manner. Mr. Bradshaw cordially thanked his co-workers, and congratulated Oughtrington Park upon winning the championship of the league. The record of the team was a splendid one, and he wls sorry Oughtrington Park would not figure in the league next season. He wished them the same suc- cess in the Manchester League. It had been alleged in newspaper correspondence that the championship of the second division had been "given" to Stockton Heath Reserve. He did not think it was necessary for him to say that that was an absolute lie. The team had fought their matches with bona -ffde reserve men and had not played first team men, as had been done in Warrington on many occasions.

The Chairman presented gold medals to Oughtringtod Park and silver medals to Stockton Heath Reserve. During the evening songs were rendered by Miss Whittle, Messrs. Pemberton, Burke, Moorfield, Thomason, Foulkes, A Pemberton, and Wright. Mr. Austen Vernon sontributed gramaphone selections, and Mr. Parry gave solos on the bones.

THE MAY QUEEN FESTIVAL. Fine Weather and a Successful Celebration.

The annual May Queen Festival was held at Earles- town on Saturday, and though the weather had been unsettled all the week the great day turned out beauti- fully fine. There was consequently a large influx ob visitors to the town, and the procession with its sus- sequent ceremonies was watched by a great concourta of people. As a gratifying result it is hoped then pearly £lOO will accrue for the St. John's Completion Wand. Decorations in the Earlestown streets were of a lavish scale.and the whole place was unmistakably and obviously en fete.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROCESSION.

The procession was led off about 2 o'clock by the Warrington Legh-street Brass Band and the mounted marshals, Messrs. W. and J. Beere. It was divided into seven sections and comprised an enormous number of characters. Miss Martha Adams was the May Queen.

HER MAJ EST Y'S ROBES.

The May Queen, who was prettily attired in a charm- ing dress of pale green Mousseline silk, gauged yoke, and finished with berthe of cream accordeon pleating. The sleeves were also gauged and finished with cream accordeon pleating. The skirt was made with a tucked front, trimmed with gold braid and strings of pearls, caught up with gold and brilliant ornaments and finished with a heavy gold tassel. The sides of the skirt were gauged, and finished with a flounce to match. The train was of rich crimson plush lined throughout with pale green Louisine silk to match the dress. This was bordered with ermine, finished on the shoulders with white plumes and cream satin ribbon, to which were attached heavy gold cords and tassels. Large bows of cream satin ribbon completed the train. The whole of the dress, &c.,were supplied by Miss A. Stathem, Haydock-street. The queen' as she was drawn through the streets in her brougham by four greys in charge of outriders, certainly made a very pretty picture, and many were the complimentary references she evoked. She carried a handsome shower bouquet of roses and lilies. Miss May Gerrard, lady-in-waiting, was attired in cream cashmere, with a train of Royal blue plush, trimmed with ermine, whilst the eight maids of honour were prettily dressed in cream cashmere, and wore dainty coronets.

BAND CONTEST.

To add to the attractions a band contest was held on the Market ground after the crowning of the " Queen," when three bands competed, viz., Earlestown, Platt Bridge, and Walkers' (Warrington). The test piece was the march " Tannhauser." Mr. Peter Fairhurst, of Crooke, was the adjudicator, and after hearing the three bands, placed Earlestown first, Platt Bridge second, and Walkers' third. The prizes were £B, .25, and .23. Pre- vious to the procession four bands competed in a quick step competition between the' Griffin Hotel and the Ram's Head Hotel. Platt Bridge took the first prize, .£2 25., Earlestown second and Legh-street, (War- rington) third, and Walker's fourth.

LOVE LETTERS FOR THE PUBLIC. A Novel Dispute at Earlestown.

A curious application came before Sir Samuel Half, in the Lancashire Court of Chancery at St. George's Hall, on Thursday morning, when Mr. Harold Mather, on behalf of a tradesman at Earlestown and his wife, sought to restrain a man named William Lundy, resid- ing in Wigan, from publishing, or parting with the possession of, a number of letters which he had in his possession, which vere the property of the plaintiffs, and which had passed between the plaintiffs prior to their marriage a short time ago. It appeared that all the parties formerly lived in one house, and the defendant thus obtained possession of the documents. Differences arose between them a few months ago, and the plaintiffs purchased a business at Earlestown. This annoyed the defendant who, with ths view of injuring the business, threatened to deliver an address outside the plaintiffs' premises and to dis- tribute in the neighbourhood the plaintiffs' private letters which had come into his possession. At the sitting of the court on Monday an order restraining the defendant front carrying out his threats was obtained. Mr. Mather now applied for a summons, returnable next Monday, preparatory to the issue of a writ of attachment for the arrest of the defendant on the ground that he was guilty of contempt in having carried out his threats in defianee of the order. Counsel read an affidavit to the effect that yesterday the defendant delivered a speech to a crowd of people outside the plaintiffs'premises, and distributed amongst them twenty-eight of the plaintiffs' letters, each being inn separate envelope tied up with ribbon. The Court, said Mr. Mather, were dealing with a desperate man. _ _

The defendant' did not appear. The Vise-chancellor granted the application, remark- ing that they would make short stork of the defendant if he disobeyed the order. He thought, however, that the defendant should be told to consult a solicitor,who might inform him that he would not be fined or would not go to gaol for fourteen days, but mighthave to go for six months or until such time as he apologised and satisfied the court that the offence would not be re- peated. He did not mean that this would necessarily be the result of the notice of motion to be dealt with on Monday, but he thought the defendant ought to be made to appreciate the importance of the situation.

Warrington Field Club.

The Warrington Field Club had their first excur- sion of the summer session on Saturday. It was well attended and fortunately the weather was fine. The party wended their way to the Denna but through the abundance of wet weather previously, there was little to attract the notice of the Zoologists generally though. the botanical world was through the same looking its best. The birch, alder, ash, holly and laburnum trees were noted in bloom, while flowers abounded. Among ethers were observed the greater stitchwort, calandine, red campion, marsh, pennywort and wood sorrel. Galls on the oak tree, a fungus on the birch, and attacks on the alder tree were some of the items that occupied the members' attention. Each collected specimens for preservation according to his special study, and altogether a pleasant afternoon was spent

THE EXAMINER.

JUNE 4, 1904.

THE Anglo-French THE ANGLO-FRENCH Convention came AGREEMENT. before the House of Commons on Wednesday, and the ,'general opinion was happily expressed by Sir EDWARD GRAY, who said he did not begrudge France what she had gained by an agreement of which the spirit was better than the letter. It is likely enough that in a material sense France has the best of the bargain. It is true that as Sir CHARLES DILKE reminded the House, certain inconvenient points have been left un- settled. We have never been able to understand why the French rights in Newfoundland were not extinguished once for all. France has sur- rendered her ownership of what was called the French shore," but she retains fishing rights in the waters of a Bricish Colony and the right to use the shore of that Colony for canning and drying fish. On the other hand we are finally rid of the friction caused by the mixed administration in Egypt. France has resigned . even the shadow of political control over a Icountry which at one time was the apple of her eye. She has given this up no doubt in exchange for the preponderent influences in Morocco to which she is justly entitled. The wisdom of this part of the agreement finds a practical illustration in the action cf the United States. An American subject has been carried oft by Moorish Brigands, and it is to France that the Washington Cabinet has turned for redress. It is no use looking to the unfortunate Sultan, who is not master in his own house. The American warships at Tangier can have more influence over the Sultan than they can over the brigands, who do not live on tha sea- shore, but France has a recognised responsibility under the Convention and is prepared to exercise it.

Wednesday's debate was rather academic

because the most important part of the subject could not be discussed. This is the effect of the agreement on the relations between England and France with regard to the war in the Far East. It would be no more judicions to talk about that in the House of Commons than to talk about it in the French Chamber. But everybody knows that the two nations have greatly relieved the danger of entanglement in the conflict between Russia and Japan. France naturally sympathises with Russia, but her sympathy is not feverish, and she shows notithe smallest disposition to question the motives of this country. It is quite easy to imagine condi- tions in which the feeling would be very different. A few years ago this crisis would have provoked angry recriminations, and made the task of pacific diplomacy in Western Europe enormously difficult ; but to-day France watches the misfor- tunes of her ally without any uneasiness as to her own position. Whatever may happen she is not called upon to lend a hand. She is not less im- pressed than the rest of the world by the revela- tion of Japan's material and moral strength, and of Russia's weakness: and she is not likely to be stirred out of her neutrality by the GERMAN EMPEROR'S alleged desire to befriend Russia in time of need. The KAISER is an incalculable fac- tor in European politics. He may still cherish the belief that Christondom ought to make common cause against the yellow races. At any rate 11(') never loses sight of the fact that Russia is his most formidable neighbour to whom it nray he politic to do a good turn. But the idea of France threaten- ing Japan with coercion in order to show that the French are gocd friends to Russia does not seem like practical politics just now.

THE DUKE of NOR-

CONSCRIPTION.

FOLK'S Commission has

not had the good fortune of the Esher Committee. When Lord ESHER and his colleagues proposed to reform the War Office root and branch, their scheme was received with pretty general approbation. The Norfolk Com- mission proposes conscription, and its Report is hailed mith a chorus of disapproval. It was ap- pointed to inquire into the efficiency of the Militia and Volunteers,and make suggestions for reorgani- sation. Suggestions it does make, but they are entirely nullified by the assertion that the Auxi hary Forces are no good in any case. As the" Standard" says, it seems absurd for the Commission to tinker with the Militia and the Volunteers when it does not believe in there. The Commissioners are not of one mind. Two or three of them diverge considerably from the chief recommendation of the majority. But there is a common bias towards the principle of compulsory military service, although they differ about its application. Tne majority would abolish the Auxiliary Forces as they now stand, and enroll half the able bodied youth of the country at the age cf twenty for the defence of this island. The DUKE OF NORFOLK and most of his colleagues are convinced that we cannot sleep safely in our beds until every young man in the household is trained to the use of arms. The Navy is all very well, but we must be prepared for the con- tingency not merely of a foreign raid on our shores but of an invasion upon a Napoleonic scale. In fine, we must take it for granted that some time or other we may lose command of the sea, and a determined foe will then seize the opportunity to land an enormous force for our subjugation. To cope with that idea the Militia and Volunteers are useless, and our young men must brace themselves for a year's regular training without barracks. It is manifest already that military reformers outside the Commission do not take this seriously. For one thing it is based on pure assumption as to the requirements of the nation for home de- fence. The Commissioners find that we need 330,000 men—a calculation for which there appears to be no warrant. It is the business of the Defence Committee to determine this point, and what their conclusion is we do not know. But it is characteristic of the haphazard methods applied to affairs of national importance that the Norfolk Commission should ignore the Defence Committee, and take upon itself a responsibility entirely foreign to its terms of reference. Well might Sir CHARLES DILKE complain, in a recent Parliamentary debate, that the administration of the Army lacks a scientific basis ! Before the country could be persuaded that even a modified form of conscription was an essential safeguard against real dangers it would need an overwhelm- ing weight of authority, military and naval,backed by the responsibility of the Executive. But the Norfolk Commission does not represent this author- ity in any sense. The Defence Committee has expressed no orinion on the subject. No great consultative body has decided what proportion of our military force is required to supplement the Navy,which,as Sir RALPH KNOX says,is ourlfirst and also our second line of defencA. Indeed, when the Commission sought advice from the Admiralty, that department, not without reason, declined to commit itself in a matter which was entirely beyond the Commission's province. It follows Ifrom all this that public opinion is not favourably impressed by the Duke of Norfolk's views. It declines to believe that the Militia and Volunteers are beyond redemption, or that the Navy is so untrustworthy as a national security that we might as well be a Continental instead of an island State. But it does believe that the chief cause of ineffici- ency is wasteful expenditure.in the administration of the Army and the incapacity or reluctance of the responsible authorities to make up their minds once for all as to the exact position England should hold as a military Power.

The National Service L 3ague is naturally pleased with the tenour of the report presented by the Royal Commission, and in anotier column prompt opportunity is taken to use tha report as a lever for raising the membership of the League. But it must not be forgotten that the Leagn) proposals involve a certain amount of compulsory service i for all young men of a stated age—or in other words conscription in a modified I form. Supporters of the League are able to see a • marked difference beween the form of service they advocate and pure unadulterated conscription, but the ordinary individual persists in considering the two things synonymous. In our opinion there is just about as much difference between the two a matter of taste. With some surprise as between butter and margarine. It is purelythat the taste of the Governinent, welind which can endure such an unpalatable dish as Chinese slavery, sticks at conscription, even in a mild form. For the pre sent it has been knocked on the head, and we rejoice thereat. The evidence last contained in the report of the Commission amply volunteer view that the la has not been said system, while further strong cor- roborative testimony s to hand from other well informed quarters. The“ Manchester Guardian- has done much to.wards forming hn intelli- gent public opinion as to the future

of the force by publishing this week valuable expressions of opinion from prominent volunteer officers. Colonel Birley, C. 8.,V. D, and Colonel Balfour, brother of the Prime Minister, are dead against conscription, and pooh pooh .the idea of compulsory service. The latter is particu- larly outspoken and condemns the scheme as " wicked nonsense." Any elaborate military train- ing for home defence he deems quite unnecessary. "To spend vast sums of money" he says" in the machinery for massing and handling huge bodies of men would be preposterous. The danger demand- ing it does not exist and never will. But it is desirable to educate young men to arms. That is the distinction I make." Colonel Edis, one of the earliest volunteers, says "Give the Volunteer force fair treatment and Ithere will be no lack of men." Most of the officers interrogated by our contemporary were strongly in favour of a separate Volunteer Department,and it seems to us that here will be found the solution to most of the difficulties under which the Volun- teer force labours. With a distinct and sympath- etic departinent at headquarters there would be hope of raising the volunte 3r force to the highest state of efficiency :required, and the conscription bogey would return to that oblivion from which it should never have emerged.

At the rate things are going on in Warrington we shall all soon become converted to socialism and the cause of labour. Worse fates might befall us as a community—in fact we infinitely prefer socialism to either protection or conscription. On Sunday evening theft, was a conference in Parr Hall on the social problem and after considerable palaver between men who are recognised thinkers as well as workers, a committee was evolved to con- sider the formation of a Social Brotherhood. The object in view is the"re-adjustment of the present social order "—which we should think will be sufficient to go on with. When the re-adjustment is complete, either the millennium or purgatory will be within measurable distance. We will hope, for the sake of the members of the new Social Brotherhood, that the progress will be in the right direction. Though we have,perhaps,spoken rather flippantly of a serious topic, we are greatly in sympathy with this movement in the ranks of Labour. It is an excellent sign that so much in- terest is being taken now-a-days in Socialogy and the uplifting of the masses, and if an amicable, whole-hearted Social Brotherhood can be formed from the reforming units in Warrington,even then something will have been accomplished which can only work for good.

There is likely to be considerable opposition at the Council meeting on Tuesday next to) Coun- cillor IVlaginn's special motion with regard to the acceptance of tenders. The militant member for St. John's Ward proposes in effect that in future the lowest tender shall be accepted by the Cor- poration Committees, subject of course to the fair 1 wage clause being observed, with the exception ' that a preference may be made in favour of local contractors. We fail to see what advantage is likely to accrue from such a resolution a,3 this. On the face of it it would cer- tainly appear that by always accepting the lowest tender the Corporation would obtain its goods and labour at the lowest market price, but , the advantage would not always be a material one. What business firm,we would ask Councillor Maginn, makes a point of always accepting the lowest tendee ? Would he himself buy a thousand chairs at 53. each if he knew that they were very much inferior to those supplied by another manu- facturer at ss. 6d. This is where the difficulty arises—and it is so obvious that we wonder at Councillor na ginn making the proposition he does. Instead of being the gainers by always accepting thp lowest tender, the Corporation Committees would frequently lose in the long run. It would be absurd to tie their hands in such a manner. They are composed of business men, and it should be left to their &scretion to decide whether the acceptance of the lowest tender is advisable or not. There are so many things to consider in fixing up a con- tract—quality of work or material, expedition in delivery and the like—that it would be supreme folly to say that the lowest tender must always be accepted. Councillor Maginn has usually been credited with a head for business, but it will need all the fluency of which he is master to conv.nce 1 the Council that there is commonsens3 in the prin- ciple underlying his motion.

Two new bye-laws of the BorpuTh of Warrington , have come into force this week. ole prohibits the disgusting habit of spitting in public places, and we hope the authlrities will make every endeav- our to prevent this loathsome practice. In the majority of cases the offenders could avoid corn - miffing a nuisance and if they will not do so of their own initiative they must be compelled. The other bye-law is aimed at a habit equally prevalent among a large section of the community. This is the striking of matches on shop-fronts and other convenient pla :es. The smoker as a rule does not care where he ignites his match, and the adjoining wall or door-way is usually, the handieit place. Such conduct is decidedly inconsiderate, but then smokers have a reputation for ignoring the feelings of others when their favourite habit is concerned. We are afraid, however' that this particular bye-law is likely to be inoperative to a great extent, for the police force would require doubling to deal with only a small proportion uf the offenders. And it would take mord than a bye-law to prevent a smoker from striking his match wherever he chooses. Shopkeepers can themselves do more in their own protection than the bye-law. A more general provision of metal strikers on shop fronts will go a long way towards remedying the nuisance.

Thi Warrington Board of Guardians had a full dress debate on Wednesday on the momentous question of whether the boys in the industrial schools are to be clothed in corduroy or tweed! The argument in favour of the substitution of tweed for corduroy was based chiefly on sentimental grounds. Corduroy it was argued was so distinctive in its character as to mark the wearer of it as a young pauper, and it was further stated that corduroy possesses a disagreeable smell. Mr. Knowles opposed the innovation on tha ground that the adoption of tweed was calculated to make the standard of the Workhouse boy's condition better than that of the 'workingman's child outside. But the matter which weighed chiefly with the Guardians was the financial aspect. When the Rev. Dr. Mercer announced that the cost would be half as much again, the proposition was as good as lost and in the end the resolution in favour of tweed was defeated by a substantial mai ority.

Local butchers, cattle dealers and others who hailed with delight the announcement of Professor Koch that human and bovine tuberculosis are not identical and almost certainly not inter-com- municable, will be disappointed at the in- terim report of the .Royal Commission appointed to inquire into the subject. The investigations of the Commission have clearly established the fact that tuberculosis is communicable from man to bovine animals. and from this it would seem to follow that the disease un is cothrnicable in the other direction also. This report would appear to place it beyond all doubt that the tubercle in the animal and the man are one and the same thing, and the publication of the Commission's findings will probably have the effect of making the regulations more string- ent respecting the sale of food calculated to menace the health of the community by contain- ing tubercle germs.

A curious little point arose at the meeting of the Runcorn Rural Council on Tuesday, when a , letter was read from the County Council pointing out that by the Motor Car Act it was obligatory on local authorities to set nip sign—posts denoting dangerous corners, cross-roads, and precipitous places. This consideration for the safety of motorists aroused the ire of Mr. T. Baxter, a well-known farmer and lover of horses. He evidently has the strong antipathy to the" teuf teuf " characteristic of the farming class all over the country, and objected to the spending of a single shilling "towards making it easy for these men to travel and run anyone down."

Mr. Baxter has evidantly failed to move with tin' times. Tne motorist has come to stay, ani though he does not always look the part, hs is fl human being and entitled to protection from the dangers of the road as much as the bumble pedestrian or the prosperous farmer with his spirited horses. It was only when the man urged upon Mr. Bixter that the Council wag obliged by law to tallsprecautionary steps for the safety of the motorists that the irate member for Dutton gave way. Chair- -6

The United States, according to our protec- tionist friends, is a paradise for the workman, where steady employment and high wages are to be relied upon. But the daily organ controlled by M. C. A. Pearson, the Chairman of the Execu- tive Committee of the Tariff Reform League' announces with great display of headlines thao fewer than 75,000 men have been dismissed by the American railways during the past two months, due to the "remarkable shrinkage of freight thal has taken place during the last year. This is the most drastic economy that has been made sicee the financial panic of 1893.' If only such a sta,te of things had been reported of Free-Trade England !

SOLICITOR AND POLICE SUPERINTENDENT. An Argument at Runcorn. An interesting betting case was heard at Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday, when Alfred Jolley, aged 17 ?rears, of Bridge-street, Runcorn, was summoned for unlawfully using and frequenting a certain street, to 'wit a public place called Irvvell-lane, for the purpose of betting or bookmaking." The defendent, who was represented by:Mr. Arthur Browne (Warrington), pleaded Not guilty. P.C. Preston gave evidence, as to seeing defendant taking slips relating to betting. Mr. Browne: What proof have you that defendant tad anything to do with the slips? Witness: I saw him take them. But they might not have been the same slips. You 'would not swear to that ?-1-le knew what I meant 'When I asked for the slips. You are leaving the Bench to infer that the slips were the same. Suppose the defendant had refused to come to the Police Statlon?—l should have searched him: By what power or right ?—For being a suspicious Person there for the purpose of betting. That is a new law to me. Have you the right to apprehend a man under those circumstances ?—I think So.

Against Spitting. The attention of the public is called to a byelaw, which has just been passed by the Warrington Cor- poration, prohibiting anyone from spitting on the floor, seat or wall of any public corridor, public hall, public waiting room or place of public entertainment. Another byelaw prohibits anyone from striking matches on shop fronts. This has been passed at the i ustigation'otthe Warrington and District Grocers' and Provision Dealers' Association, who have complained of the damage done to the fronts of business pre.nises by the practice of striking matches thereon. The Stockton Heath Tramway Extension. The last day for the receipt of tenders for the lay- ing of steel rails, paving and forming the permanent way of the tramway extension from Wilderspool to Victoria-square, Stockton Heath,is Tuesday, June 14th. It is expected that the work will be comnienced within a month. The crossing of the swing bridge presents an engineering difficulty with reference to the allign- meat of the bridge after opening. Mr. Hunter,engineer for the Manchester Ship Canal Company, and Major Cardew consulting electrical engineer for the Cor- poration, had a conference on the _spot this week, and it is believed that the steps proposed to be taken will-overcome the difficulty. The Killing of Wild Birds. An order called the County Borough of Warring- ton Wild Birds' Protection Order was inserted in the " Lond ni Gazette" on Tuesday, and has been issued by the Home Office, extending the provisions of the Wild Birds' Protection Act to a large number of other birds not previously scheduled lathe Act. The order also prohibits the destruction of any birds on the river Mersey within the Borough. The general effect is that the Wild Birds' Protection Act which pro- hibits the taking and destroying of certain birds from the Ist March to the Ist August, is extended in the Warrington order to nearly all birds during the same period. With regard to the river Mersey, the order prohibits the killing of birds at any time during the year. This order prevents anyone from shooting on the river at any birds within the confines of the

Borough.

Honour for Mr. J. Rymer Young. An honour was on Wednesday conferred upon Mr. J. Rymer Young, of Warrington, who was elected Vice-President of the Pharmaceutical Society of Great Britain which is a very important and influential body. The election was by ballot, and in announcing the result the President (Mr. R. A. Robinson) said he had the pleasure of congratulating Mr. Young, and inviting him to take the vice-chair.—Mr. Young, in acknowledging his election, thanked the Council for placing him in a position whicb• had attached to it enormous responsibili- ties, as well as honour and distinction. He was perfectly well aware that the honours of the Society were not placed upon individuals as marks of honour only. He fully recognized that fact, and he was compelled to the conclusion that in placing him in the office of vice-president the Council was of opinion that he could be of some use to the society. It would be his duty and endeavour to justify the confidence of the Council in him. He asked the council for a continuance of that support and forbearance which had usually been accorded to .the occupants of the chair and vice-chair, and without which those offices could not be pleasurable, nor even tolerable.—Mr. Young has been a member of the council since 1893, and his election will meet with universal approval, and give great satisfa ctiou to his fellow townsmen. FIRE GRATES of every description.—J. S. GAR- LAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, and 53, Bridge-street,Warringion.— -Advt. New Catholic Church. Sunday, June 19th, will witness the solemn opening of the new church of St. Benedict's, Orford lane, by his Lordship the Abbot of Ampleforth. The new church is a corrugated iron building of pleasing design, providing accommodation for 400 worshippers. It has been erected by Mr. J. Me. Manus, of Hammersmith, London, and is now nearing completion. The main entrance is from Earle-street,immediately behind St. Benedict's schools. The interior is of white vanished wood and the church is well lighted and airy. Accommodation is provided for the choir and there is a baptistry, a sacristry and a confessional. The work of seating and providing the altar rails has been placed in the hands of Mr. Rigby. The church is nearing completion, and will be quite ready for the opening service. Services have been held during the past eighteen months in St. Benedict's schoolroom, which has been utilised as a school chapel. The Rev. Father Baines is the priest in charge and it is mainly through his efforts that the new church has been erected.

THE MARRIAGE OF MISS LYON. A Versatile Bride.

On Tuesday next the marria4e will be solemnized of Lord Acton, secretary at the Berne Legation, and Miss Lyon, only daughter of Mr. T. H. Lyon, of Appleton Hall. The ceremony will take place at Brompton Oratory. "The great London welding of neat week" says "The Sketch," "is that of Lord Acton, the good-looking son of a fatuous father, and Miss Lyon, the daughter of a popular Cheshire squire. The future Lady Acton is a girl after her celebrated father-in-law's heart, for she is extraordinarily versatile,having great musical and literary tastes, while yet as fond of outdoor life and pursuits mare most of her contemporaries. Miss-Lyon is an expert tennis-player and golfer, and at Cannes her fame as a tennis-player used to bring crowds of French visitors to the club every time she played. The marriage will bring together the political, the diplo- matic and the Roman Catholic worlds,and the Brompton Oratory is admirably adaptel to be the scene of a great wedding. There will be a group of pretty brides- maids including Lord Acton's sister and the Duke of Miss Elspeth Campbell, and both their Ag or wg ynlsl 'as nd niece, the wedding-dress will revive the becoming modes worn at the Court of Marie Antoinette." The bride's dress,"says "The Onlooker" "is of Marie Antoinette days, with floating train of beautiful lace, and she will wear the pearl necklace given her by Lord Acton. The bridesmaids include Lord Acton's sister, as well as four cousins of the bride, and Miss Elspeth Campbell, Lady Elizabeth Northcote, and others. They will also wear Marie Antoinette frocks and the 'pretty torquoise and pearl and diamond monogram brooches i given to them by the bridegroom. Some beautiful presents have been received by Miss Lyon, including a tiara of diamonds from her mother, and a joint gift in the form of an emerald and diamond pendant from Mrs. Thorpe and others. A beautiful photograph in an Italian marble frame is from the Princess of Bourbon; the Countess of Cassillis has given a silver dish, Lady Violet Poulett an enamel box Lady Mary Pepys a beautiful old Holy Water stoup, Lady Hope a gold box, Lady Hood of Avalon some silver, and M. Maurice de Seynes, Secretary at the French Enibassy, is among the many others who have sent gifts." THE LOCAL CELEBRATIONS. Arrangements are being made by the tenants and others on the Appleton estate to give the couple a warm welcome on their arrival after the honeymoon at Appleton Hall on Thursday, June 23. A reception will b.. held in the grounds when the tenantry and others will present the bride with a s diamond bracelet, while the gardeners and workmen on the estate will give her a pearl and sapphire brooch. On the following day the joyful festivities will be continued. On this occasion the school children will attend, and the members of the Primrose League, when, it is expected, further presenta- tions will be made.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, June 4, 1904

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.]

methods. The report of that deputation was published some time ago by our Board of Agriculture, and it is well worth reading. It seems that so far as the cost of production is con- cerned, there is little difference between Holland and England. The Dutchman pays a higher rent as a rule, but he kas fixity of tenure, and freedom of cultivation. His rent averages about ,C 5 Ds. to 23 an acre, and his wages bill is nearly as high as oar own. So he has to pay a heavy toll to his landlord, and he tries to punish the middleman for it. He combines with his neigh- bours and forms a co-operative society for the sale of produce. The goods are packed in cases, bearing the grower's own trade mark, and they are inspected to see that they are all honestly packed. If any member is caught "topping," or putting the biggest and best fruit on the top, and small rotten ones at the bottom he is fined for the first offence. If he does it a second time, his name will be put on the" black list " for a week or two, and the society will refuse to take his produce. That will be a serious matter for him, because he will not only lose his market, but he will be advertised as a bit of a rogue. Members of this society pay a sabscription of less than two shillings a vearean 1a c nami sion of one or two per cent. The goods are sold by auction for cash, and they are paid for to the producer every week, so he has no undercutting to fear, and he Make 3 no bad debts, and he has not to wait long for his money. %The English farmer might learn a thing or two if he would about marketing in bulk, and getting his goods in the market cheaply, and quickly. The enterprising Dane markets his eggs the day they are laid, and his milk is creamed as sloe as it s coils 1, by these co- operative agencies, and he gets nal every week for his produce. The Dat,chnian's c reinf)eri and vegetables are sent off the day they are sold, aal he gets his money at the Week-end. The English fanner understands auction marts for cattle and sheep, and horses, and that is about as far as he has got. Some day he will ask whether it is worth his while to take his stuff to the market himself, and waste a day over it, and probably get drunk into the bargain. New ideas travel slowly, and improved methods are not readily adopted, but we may lea.rna good deal from the Dutch cultivator, and his up-to-date methods. We have greater natural advantages, but he is wider awake than we are. It is about time we wakened up and be- stirred ourselves. How strangely history repeats itself. Many centuries ago the Flemish settlers in this country brought their carrots and cabbages, and celery, and hops, and ever so many of our familiar fruit trees, and flowers, and planted them in our gardens. They taught us how to grow root crops to feeds our cattle In the winter. They taught us all we knew about spinning, and weaving, and earthen- ware, and mining. We may very well sit at their feet once more, and learn the mysteries of intense culture, and profitable dairy farming. It is no use seeking success in old-fashioned ideas, and obsolete methods, and then clamouring for protection against those wicked Duchmen, who are" dumping" all manner of good things at our feet. The world moves, and we must keep he pace, or we shall be trampled under men's feet.
