SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN.

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of " Search Lights on, Land, Llbour

and Social Problems."

===== 0002601_19040604_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DUTCH CULTIVATOR.

"If you want to know what agriculture can be, and what can be grown on a given analunt of soil, you must apply for information to the faraors of Flanders and Jersey, and Lombardy. Or else you may ask the market gardeners of Ho KROPOTKIN. In these days of Labour Registries, and Allotments, and small holdings, it is interesting to know what other nations are doing to bring back the people to the land, and enable them to earn their own living by cultivating the soil. All civilised nations admit that it is not a _

good thing to crowd our workmen into big towns and oities, and encourage them to work in factories, while the agricultural villages and farms. aold fields are de- serted. So our Social Reformers everywhera a-e cry-

in—g-:l3aa- to the Land," and they are findin w k Cnr .our unemployed in markat gardens, and small far ni and

orchards:

Prince Kropotkin has been studying these movements all over Western Europe, and in his interesting book, "Fields, Factories, and Workshops," he gives us some delightful object lessons from his own observation. I advise every Social Reformer to buy that book and read it. There is a cheap edition that only costs one shilling. I. TEIE DUTCII CULTIVATOR TRIUNIPIIED OVER NATURAL LXtS.DVANPAGE. _ Whenever you advocate a settlement of labour on the

land, you meet with some croaker who raises a host of objections. The soil is wrong, and the climate is un- suited, and the situation is objectionable, and it will require too much capital to work it. Let every such croaker read what the Dutch cultivator has done, aid then go and do likewise, or ever after hold his peace. For many centuries Holland has had land and labour

problems Of her own, that kept her wide awake, and called forth all her energies, and gave her no peace. The country is very flat and diffi.cult to drain, and many of its fields and meadows are below the level of _ _

the S'ea. The coast is protected by artificial barriers and embankments, to keep the sea out, but every now and then some great storm will sweep away these ram- parts, and floods will rush over the low-lying fields, and drown the people like rats in their holes. . _ . _ _ _

So the stOlid, phlegmatic Dutchman is everlastingly at war with the devouring sea, and every time it robs him, he tries to go one better, by filching a few thou- sand acres of foreshore from it. With infinite patience and dogged perseverance, he builds walls of sand and earth, round the shallow shore he claims as his own,and when he has made it strong, he builds windmills on the embankment, and he pumps all the water back again into the sea. He plants pine trees, and hardy bushes, and start grass, on his enbankments, and makes them so strong that they can resist any storms or floods that may come.

the whole country is covered with canals, and water- ways built after this fashion, and there are windmills everywhere to drain the low-lying land and pour the water into these canels. If all these canals were _ _

stretched out into a line they would Wa..C-1; Sixty times round the world.

Now I think you will agree with me that Holland is not an ideal country for any ram to make a living in by farming., or market gardening. It offers no advan- ..

tige of soil, or situation, or climate, and it is about the last country in the world that a practical cultivator would choose for the investment of his labour,and skill, and capital. Somebody has said that when the Dutch farmer goes forth to sow, he carries his seed basket in one hand, anda bucket in the other, for he has to bail out the water before he can find any soil to drop his seed in and bury it. . .

And yet, witti all thee natural disadvantages, and in spite of incgedible difficulties, the Dutch cultivator has worked some stupendous miracles, and he has shown us what agriculture can be, and how it can be made to maintain the people in comfort. In other words, his brains and science and persistent labour, have triumphed over every obstacle in his path. IL—IGRICULTURE IS TaE DUTCaNIAN'S LAST RESOURCE.

He took to that trade when all other trades failed, and he has made it pay. I know no nation that has had more ups and downs than Holland. Nature intended its people to be fishermen, and sailors, and three or four centuries ago they had a mighty fleet, and nearly all the carrying trade of the world was in their hands, but we destroyed their ships, and captured their commerce, Then they took to spinning and hand-loom weaving, and manufacturing, but the invention of the steam engine stranded them once more, and they lost those industries. Then they took to erowing wheat, and oats, and barley, till foreign nations could grow these things mare cheaply, and then they changed their employment again. All these changes are characteristic of the people. If you drive a Dutchman out of one trade, he will mighty soon find another. When he found that it did not pay to grow wheat, and barley, and oats, in competition with other nations, he quietly dropped those unprofit- able game% and turned his attention to dairy farming and market gardening. H 3 knew he would have to compete with nations more favourably circumstanced than himself, and with better opportunities of reaching the best market, so he called science to his aid, and he produced some surprising results. Prince Kropotkin in criticising the methods of these Dutchmen says: "They have created a totally new agriculture. They smile when we boast about the rotation system having permitted us to take from the field one crop every year, or four crops in three years, because it is their ambition to have six,and nine crops,from the very same plot every twelve months. They do not understand our talk about bad soils, because they make the soil themselves, and make it in such quantities as to be compelled yearly to sell some of it, or it would raise up the level of their gardens by half an inch every year. We raise five or six tons of grass to the acre, but they raise from fifty to a hundred tons of various vegetables on the same quantity of land. While we are raising five pounds worth of hay they are getting one hundred pounds worth]of cabbages, and carrots, and other vegetables."

These siirprising results can only be produced by inten culture, and the creation of suitable soil, and the use ol chemical fertilisers. Our own agricultural colleges, mid County Council Schools, bring these mysteries within the reach of a few educated young people, who oan afford time and money to attend their classes. The Dutchman carries this precious knowledge to every village and hamlet in the land, and puts it freely within and each of everybody. And he not only preaches, but he practises. He sets apart Certain plots of land in farming centres for practical experiments, to show the farm labourers what science can do to help them. He calls these plots" proof fields," and he gives practical illustrations of his methods before all the people, and then invites them to watch the results day by day. He also employsa large staff of "wandering teachers" or lecturers on dairying, agriculture, and kindred sub- jects, to go;into all the country districts, and give free, , instruction to old and young. So knowledge is spread- ing and new ideas are taking root, and improved methods are beingadopted all over the country. Prince Kropotkin tells us that the Dutch cultivators have not only produced some wonderful crops, and bountiful harvests, but they have distinguished them- selves in cattle-rearing. Some men have an idea that one kind of a cow is as good as another, and if she has four legs, and a head,and a tail, she is all right. The Dutch farmer knows better. He knows that some cows are hardly worth their keep, while others are very profitable investments. So he buys the best cow in the market, because the best is cheapest,andlno matteilhow good she may be he tries to make her better. His flocks and herds have increased considerably during the last quarter of a century, and he has produced cows that yield more milk and butter than any others in the world. It is a Dutch cow that holds the record of three hundred and twenty pounds of butter in a year as her yield, and it is a record that will take some beating. You need not be surprised when I tell you that our own English buyers will go over te Holland and give better prices for Dutch butter and dairy produce, and cattle, than they will give in our own markets. The Dutch also grow early cucumbers;in frames, and they send them hero so cheaply that many of our own gardeners have ceased to grew them. They send us such quantities of potatoes, onions, beetroot, pickling cabbages, and kitchen vegetables, that our own market gardeners are complaining of their competition, andask- ing for protection. 'These are all very wonderful results when you consider the circunistances under which they are produced. Holland is only about half the size of Ireland, and she has to feed nearly twice as many people. She not only feeds her own children with all the vegetables and dairy produce they need, but she sends to us millions of pounds worth of food every year. When you contrast, these two countries you will see that a nation's pros- perity does not depend so much on its soil, or climate,or situation, as on the character of its people. lII.—THE DUTCH FAKA:ER P.BLIETES-4 Iri CO-OPERATION.

• He produces perishable crops, that must be on the I market soonafter they,,are cut, and gathered. It would not pay him to send a large number of small parcels to England, so he combines with his fellow cultivators and they send their crops in bulk. This is a plan that the English cultivator noes not really adopt, but he will be driven to it one of these days. At present he growls at his rivals and says unparliamentary things about them. Some time ago the onion growers round Ilggleswade, in Bedfordshire, accused them of employing cheap, sweated labour, and competing unfairly with them in growing onions. WARRINGTON GRAMMAR

SCHOOL.

A NEW DEVELOPMENT.

The governors of the Warrington Grammar School have taken the important step of making application to the Board of Education for the recognition of the school as a secondary school B. This decision was arrived at on Tuesday after- noon when a meeting was held to consider the report of the inspectors at the recent examination. The examiners recommended the governors to make the application and there is not the least doubt but that the Board of Education will accede to the request. In becoming a secondary school B under the new act the Grammar School will be a recognised educational institution in the town for higher education. The secondary school now conducted by the Education Com- mittee at the Technical Institute is known as a second- ary school A and the Grammar School as a secondary school B will take more advanced subjects, including higher science, higher mathematics and classics. One advantage gained will be the receipt of Government grants. Hitherto the Grammar School has relied solely for its income on endowment and the fees of pupils. The Government grants will enable the governors to increase the staff, improve the appointments of the school, and promote greater efficiency* Already a scheme is under consideration for providing an additional laboratory for physics and a new lectare room. These very desirable im- provements can be effected without embarking on build- ing operations. A wing of the building was originally set apart for the accommodation of boarders,but all the boys are now day students and it is proposed to convert the disused dormitories into a lecture room and a physics laboratory. The school is already provided with a well-appointed chemical laboratory, and when the pro- posed improvements are effected the school will be more efficiently equipped for its important work. At the present time there are 87 day students, but it is hoped to increase the number to 100. The Grammar School has a long and interesting his- tory, and in view of the step taken by the governors a glance into its past will not be out of place. The school is indebted for its foundation to Sir Thomas Boteler, one of the heroes of Flodden, whose knightly family were the lords of Warrington from a period very shortly after the Conquest. By his will, dated 16th August, 12 Henry VIII. (1520), after reciting that he had " delyvit by indenture tripartede into the custody and keepyng of the righte revende Father in God, John Abbottey, of Whalley, fyve hundrethe markes in golde savely to be kepte to his use and to be disposede at his pleasure," he declares that "it is his full will and mynde that his executors should have the disposicon and order- yng of the saidsurn of fyve hundrethe markes to pur- chase and obteyne lands, tents or rents to the yerely value of ten pounds above all charges or as myche there of as should be unprovidett and purchasede by him, and therewith to found a fre gram scole in Weryngton to endure for ev." Sir Thomas died on the 27th April, 1522; and his pious intentions were carried into effect by an indenture dated the 16th April, 1526. to which date the foundation of the school is referred. The deed was made" between Thomas Boteler, Esquire, son and heir of Sir Thomas Boteler, of the first part, Dame Margret Boteler,late wife of ye said Sir Thom, Ranulph Pole Clarke, Richard Sneyde, Esq., and William Plumtre Chapleyn, executors of the testament and last will of ye said Thomas Boteler of the second part, Sir Richard Bolde, knight, and 15 other knights, esquires, and gen- tlemen, four of whom had married daughters of the testa.tOr„ the intended trustees (or feoffees as they were then styled) of the third part, and Sir Richard Taylor clerk, named, deputed and ordained schoolmaster of a new free school made and ot dallied by those present at Warrington in ye county of Lancashire of ye fourth part." Since boys first entered its portals nearly four hun- dred years ago the school has;centinuouslylcarried on its educational work in Warrington. Its surroundings were for a long number of years entirely rural, in striking contrast to its present environment of factories and working-class dwellings. It became one of the most important educational institutions in South-West Lancashire and boarders came to it from long distances. The boys attending it to-day belong to the immediate district and no .boarders are accepted, but the manage- ment and reputation of the school were never better. Many boys have made the school the stepping-stone to higher positions in the scholastic world and have afterwards made their mark in various walks of life. The present headmaster, Rev. E. J. Willcocks M.A. has been connected with the school since 1860, for the first ten years as second master and afterwards, as headmaster. He is very popular with the boys and his long experience enables him to advise them wisely as to their future careers.

The Clergy Daughters' School. It is settled at last that this institutiln, which has existed for sixty years in Warrington since its founda- tion by the Hon. and Rev. Horace Powys in 1844, then Rector of Warrington, shall be removed. The great step is to be taken in the summer holidays, so the next anniversary on Wednesday, July 27th, will be the last. It is with very great regret, says the " Warrington Parish Church Magazine," that we shall say good-bye, for we have taken the very deepest in- terest in its welfare. We cannot conceal from our- selves that brighter surroundings in a lovely and healthy part of Derbyshire will be of great benefit to girls who have to spend some ten years of youthful life in the acquisitir of a valuable education, though we believe that the advantages of a situation acces- sible to the three dioceses and the privilege of worship in a large and 'beautiful church close to the school have been very great. Our connection with the school during the past sixteen years has been.of the closest, and we shall watch with affectionate in- terest its future career, which under its talented Head Mistress, we trust, will be one of great prosperity and much blessing to the daughters of the Church.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Warrington are to be congratulated upon their brilliant victory at Ashton-under-Lyne. It was no mean achievement to trounce so powerful an organiza- tion, and the speedy dismissal of the home team was sensational, not to say unexpected. The Warring- tonians maintain their unbeaten record—and one mare is to be aided to a list of creditable perform- ances. It is h.opad—and not without good grounds— that this season will ba remembered as highly successful. The team is a strong one, and so well have batsmen and bowlers acquitted themselves, that the prospects are certainly encouraging.

Batsmen and fielders shone in Saturday's match, which was remarkable for low scoring. Warrington had first .nnings, but owing to the state of the wicket and the capital bowling, runs were difficult to get. The side was dismissed fir 50, Lynes being top scorer with IS. T. Wright was also in fine form and placed a faultless 15 to his credit. W. Richardson, who was responsible for 10, was the only other bats- man to attain double figures. The bowling of Hewetsan and Bailey gave the Warrington men a great deal of trouble. Hewetson captured five wickets for 19 runs, and Bailey took 5 for 31.

The early collapse of the Ashton eleven came as a surprise. Disaster followed disaster, and the team could make little headway against the deliveries of Lynes and E. A. Connor. Within 30 minutes all the wickets were down, and not one player ran into double figures. E. A. Connor accounted for three wickets at a cost of 7 runs, and Lynes captured five for 14.

Warrington 2nd were in good form, but failed to achieve victory over the Ashton second contingent. A draw was the result of the game the teams being pretty evenly matched. E. Pierpoint batted in fine style for 32, and A. Thewlis gave an excellent display for 24. The ex-Warrington professional also distin- guished himself with the ball, and in his first nine avers—eight of which were maidens—he captured 2 wickets for a single run.

A remarkably good bowling performance by Gleave was the feature of the match between Sankey Wire Mills and Knotty Ash. Seven wickets for 9 runs was a capture of which any player might be proud. Knotty Ash had to admit defeat by 38, their best batsman being Jump, who compiled 16. For the Wire Mills, Clarke headed the list with 31.

Cheshire Lines Engineers had the hardest of luck at Arley, the villagers beating them by a single run. For the victors, Egerton, Warburton and J. V. Smith each scored 23, whilst Brown made a splendid not out innings of 36 for the engineers. The most suc- cessful bowlers for the engineers were J. Whitham taking 3 wickets for 7 and Duckworth 3 for 11. Al- together the game was one of the most exciting ever played by the engineers.

Weaverham were more than a ro.atcb for Crosfield's, and triumphed by a margin of nine. Not a solitary batsman reached double iigu res.

Penketh had to bend the knee to Widnes White Rose who ran up a total of 52 against 31 There was no very tall scoring on either side, 13pson's innings for Penketh, which produced 19, being the best per- formance.

Latchford Baptists made short work of Stretton and Appleton who could only raise the miserable score of 16. The Baptists responded with 60, Fletcher con- tributing 11.

St. Paul's had the best of their engagement with Penketh Friends' School. Sixteen was the majority in their favour, and they were greatly assisted by W. Lawless who scored 17. For Penketh, J. Waites managed to go just one better.

Moore and Daresbury played an interesting game with Lostock Gralam, and ran out winners by 6 runs. Lymm triumphed over Ashley by a 45 majority.

Walker's Brewery 2nd suffered defeat at the hands of the "Guardian" printers who had capital batsmen in Gelderd, Woodhead, and Hassey. Burrell cap- tured six wickets for 3 runs. Gregory was responsible for five wickets for 20, and Darton br four for 16 for the Brewery.

There was quite a Derby day at Newton when the Earlestown eleven tried conclusions with the local welders of the willow. Earlestown won somewhat easily, Barr heading the score list with 32. Rylance bowled splendidly for Newton towards the finish, tak- ing four wickets With five balls.

Grappenhall St. Wilfred's just averted defeat, their opponents from Orford Barracks being in great form. The military men had no difficulty in compiling 126, and when six wickets were down they declared their innings. Grappenhall fared badly, and had scored 51. with only one wicket to fall when the time limit was reached. Lieutenant Wheen rattled up 32, and Sergeant Cullerton made 31. not out.

Fairfield secured their fifth victory by defeating Sankey Wire Hills 2nd. The wicket was a very diffi- cult one to score on, and Fatrfield had their work cut out in making the total of 5.2 runs. However when their visitors came in to bat they fared much worse, for owing to the splendid bowling of A. E. Boardman, who secured the -splendid average of 7 wickets for 2 runs, they only made the total of 7 runs. To-day Fairfield visit St. Helens Cable Co. when a good game is anticipated.

The opening main of the season will be played at tile Liberal Club this afternoon. The green is in splendid condition, and some capital games are anticipated.

Saracen's Head entertained Winnington Park on Saturday and came out victorious by a majority of 70 in singles and 27 in doubles. The Junior Conserva- tives had a close encounter with Rylands Bros. In the singles, Rylands's gained a majority of seven, while the Conservatives had the best of the dxibles, three being the margin in their favour.

The competition for the cup presented to the Prince of Wales L-xl4ri of Oddfellows by Mr. A. H. Oros- field will take place on August 6th. Other prizes will be offered, and the event is expected to be a great SUCCelle.

Bowling.

• Week by week the interest in the works competition increases. The attendance on the King and Queen green on Saturday was larger than ever, about 300 enthusiasts being present in the course ch the after- noon. Three capital games were witnessed and only one first round engagement now remains to be decided As was anticipated, Ocford Tannery had the best of the match with Naylor's timber works. Only one of Naylor's players beat his opponent, and that was J. Howarth, the captain, who placed four more points to his credit than T. Bibby. Orford have a strong, team, and ran up well last year. They are expected to give a good account of themselves in the later stages of the present competition.

Howley Tannery ran Smith's Wire Works very close but had to admit defeat by 3 points. Ditchfield, the tannery captain, went off with great dash, and beat H. Massey by 15 points to two. The W;re Works' captain. (11. Daintith) was also victorious. Of the 12 games the wins were equally divided, and excitement ran high. Greening's had little difficulty in disposing of Whitecross No. 3by a 65 majority. They won 9 games but there were one or two close contests. T. Drew defeated. J. Lomax by 15 to 12, and R. Lomax secured a victory-over F. Page by 15 to 11.

To-day the concluding match of the first round will be played between Wilderspool, Iron Works and Stubs's file works, and I fancy Stubs's will run out winners. In the second round, Thewlis's file works will meet Cheshire Lines, and Cockhedge factory will try conclusions with Locker's Wire Works. Victory will in all probability rest with the railway men and Cockbedge. Of course, Wilderspool and Locker's are both new teams and there may be a sur- prise in store.

There should be a splendid match on Monday even- ing between Pearson and Know lea No. 1 and Fletcher Russell's. Fearson and Knowles' is a fine team, captained by Mr. George Ball, jun., a well-known Lancashire county player and may be relied upon to make the most of its chances—which are very rosy. They • have a

good reputation, and some fine games sbould be wit- nessed. The time for the opening is 0.30.

Greenings, referee, Mr. Holbrook. There should be a close match between the two last-named teams, and it would be difficult to forecast the result.

On June 18th Pearson and Knowles No. 2 will •be pitted against Landon and North-Western Railway, and Orford Tannery will give battle against Hough- ton's Wire Works. The railway team carried off the cup last year, and their chances for the second round appear particularly bright. Orford and Houghton's will be sure to strain every nerve to gain the mastery, and the matches for the day should be interesting enough for any enthusiast.

The success which has attended the Warrington and District League has amply justified the clubs in embarking upon the venture. The matches are being contested;vvith spirit, and the league is certainly giving an impetus to the healthful game in Warrington and district. Bull's Head achieved a fine performance on Saturday in winning awayfor the second time. They are the first organization which has met with success away this season. Brooklands' were their opponents and had to admit defeat to the extent of 35 points. Bull's Head won 9 games, and there were some excit- ing stages, a single point beinr, the margin in three contests. Liberal Club triumphed on their own green Chapel. House being defeated by 50 points. The Liberals were in grand form and won 10 games. They are hoping that this will be the forerunner of many successes. A capital game was that between E. J. Mitchell and J. Sparkes, the first-named running out winner by 2 points.

Cycling.

There is very little to chronicle this week, the un- settled state of the weather having interfered with many outings: Saturday, however, was • a splendid day for cycling, and the Warrington Club run to Chester was thoroughly enjoyed. The roads were in excellent order and eleven members journeyed per bike. They were joined by a car-load of four, making a pleasant little party. Tea was served at the Hop Pole, and a stroll round the ancient city and a walk by the Dee helped to while away the time most agreeably. A. grand run home brought a capital outing to a close.

Owing to the incessant downpour,Taesdayl.evening's run to Halton, with its old castle, had reluctantly to be abandoned. The same course had to be taken on Wednesday evening which should have witnessed the initial round of the first b3wlillg main. The event, which is being anticipated with interest and pleasure, will take place on Wednesday next.

To-day's run is to Cranage, and on Tuesday the club goes to that favourite resort—Warburton.

A dozen members of Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Club had a successful outing to Liverpool. Some crossed the river and spent a good tirnei at New Brighton whilst others contented th?:n3elves with viewing the sights of the city.

MOTOR NOTES.

A simple and yet a thoroughly reliable petrol gaugo is sadly wanted. Nothing is more annoying than tg be deceived concerning the amount of petrol remaininy in the tank, and trouble upon this score could very easily be minimised with the advent of some reall - practicable apparatus for determining the precise con dition of the petrol reservoir. 'Mere are, it is true, one or two devices upon the market which are destined to meet tbeir need, but up to the present I have not managed to find one that has proved actually satis- factory.

The majority of the 1901 patterns of motor bicycles are remarkable for the increased h.p. of their engines, , the most powerful yet built being a four-cylinderedl 8 h.p. machine which was shewn by Mr. Charles' Binks at the recent exhibition. This, however, was quite exceptional, although his 41 four-cylindered cycle is a familiar object upon the roads around Nottingham. Most of the makers, however, have designed their machines with engines 3 h.p. and 31 h.p. and these prove to b extremely fast upon the road. There is no doubt that the higher powered motors have been speedily popularised by the advent of the various types of carriage attachments which have come so prominently to the fore during the last twelve months, and undoubtedly, if the cycle is to be used mainly in connection with one of these, an engine of fairly high h.p. practically becomes a necessity. For 'the needs of a single rider, however, the h.p. of some of theonew machines seems to be too great for Use in a land where a twenty miles an hour speed is the , utmost allowed by the law.

Of the carriage attachments, the fore-carriage seems to be the most popular at present, although, person- ally, I prefer the side-carriage, for mechanical reasons as well as for the matter of sociability. Provided it is properly coupled to the 'machine, the side strain should not be particularly severe, although, of course, much depends upon the manner in which the coupling attachment is made. And with the carriage at the side instead of the being at front, the danger of the motor overheating is eliminated, whilst conversation between the driver and the driven one is comfortably possible—a state of affairs that seldom exists with the fore-carriages, and that is utterly impossible with the traler, which, by the way, appears to be practically a thing of the past.

better protected than is at present the case, and yet, even on a Pedalled bicycle, it is quite usual to see the chains rim naked, although it is obvious that the perpetual deposits of mud must alter the pitch of the chain, and perhaps eventually cause it to run off the cogs.

In the same way, the vital parts of the delicate machinery of a car are most often left exposed to all the mud and dust thrown up by the road. It is true that on some of the more superior makes, such as the Sunbeam and the Darraci, this is prevented, by the adoption of a modification of the device used originally upon the Dacauville. In this the mechanism rests upon a bed-plate of metal, which is bolted to the sides of the frame. Yet a little reflection will show that what is bad for the comparatively simple meohanical arrangements of a bicycle must be ruina- tion indeed for the intricate working parts of a car. A. temporary shield •should always be devised to lessen this evil as much as passible, or in the case Of a new car being ordered, special instructions should be given for some 'description of gear case to be fitted.

The London to Edinburgh motor-cycle ride, arranged by the ,London Motor-Cycle club, excited considerable interest in the Scottish capital, and there was a large gathering at the General Post-office on Saturday evening to witness the finish. The riders to the number of 46, started at 10 o'Clock on Friday night, and gold medals were offered to those who suc- ceeded in reaching Edinburgh within 24 hours. Shortly before eight o'clock mile riders arrived in a bunch, having completed the journey within 22 hour from the start. Others put in an appearance at in ervals until 10 o'clock, the number qualifying for the old medal being 22. The recent successful run of motor cyclists from. London to Edinburgh has served more than one use- ful purpose. It has proved beyond any shadow of I doubt, the absolute reliability of the motor cycle as a touring vehicle—always providing, of course, that it has been turned out by some reputable firm, and not merely "assembled " from parts by an indifferent engineering mephanic. It has also shewn that, not- withstanding its obvious drawbacks, the belt still re- mains the most popular form of transmission, fully three-fourths of the riders getting through being so equipped. And finally, it has demonstrated that, for real organisation in a matter of this kind, it is necessary to invoke the help of some old long distance road rider, who knows the ropes,understands a rider's needs and grasps the best way in which he may pro- vide the fullest satisfaction with the least amount of worry for the already more or less worried com- petitors.

The Automobile Club appears to be quite helpless in the matter of dealing with inconsiderate or reckless driving. One of the cpmpetitors in the London to Edinburgh ride was knocked off his *le by a motor car on the wrong side of the road near the summit of Digswell Hill—a point where it is certainly not wide enough to take a large car at top speed in the dark- ness, even if the proper rule of the road is observed. He was thrown upon a heap of stones and rendered temporarily unconscious; but neither driver nor passengers troubled themselves in the least concern- ing his welfare, and the car was driven straight on. It is acts of hooliganism such as this that bring dis- credit upon the sport and assist in promoting restric- tive legislation which presses rather hardly upon those who habitually drive temperately and with a proper respect for the rights and privileges of other users of the highway.

The French trials for the best team to represent France in the Gordon Bennett Race have resulted in the following selection ; Thery I. (" Richard Brasier ") ; Salleron, 2 (" Mors") ; and Rougier, 3. (" Turcat-Mery "). Gabriel, on aDe Dietrich, was a poor fourth and will be the reserve. Jarrott suffered misfortunes with his carburetter ; but for all that he seemed to have but little chance from quite an early period in the contest. They drove consistently well throughout, doing the fastest time in every circuit. This performance has come as a great sur- prise to many not intimately conversant with the doings of the automobile world ; and he is even referred to, in more than one quarter, as an" unheard-of racer." But, so far from this actually being the case, his pluck and skill in , driving have long been recognised. When, last year he drove a Decauville in the ill-fated Paris-Madrid Race, that terminated so abruptly and tragically, he was in a splendid position when the race was officially stopped, notwithstanding that be had been delayed by the various calamities on the road and had but narrowly escaped sharing the fate that befel poor Marcel Renault. It was in trying to overtake Thery, that Renault collided with the tree and brought about the injuries which caused his death ; the marvel is that Thery was not also involved in the smash, and it is.obvions that nothing but his coolness and nerve saved him. This is the type of man to make a suc- cessful Gordon-Bennett driver.

One cannot help feeling some regret at the ill-luck which seems to be pursuing the Darracq cars just now. Although by some thought to be the fastest of any of the cars entered•for England, the Isle of Man Trials showed that they were not really ready for the tests. In France on the other hand, they appear to have been in a completed condition, but, nevertheless, they have, one and all, been eliminated. This makes six Darraca cars in all built for the Gordon-Bennett and they have been beaten- in the preliminary trials-

WALKING DAY ARRANGEMENTS.

All the Sunday school officials in the town are busily engaged in making the arrangements for the annual scholars festival, which is to be held on Friday, July Ist. Many of the Nonconformist schools will as usual, proceed by train to some delightful spot in the neigh- bourhood, after parading some of the streets of the town. There will be organised processions of the Church of England scholars and Roman Catholic scholars in the morning.

In the "Warrington Palish Magazine " the following particulars are given of the arrangements which have been made :—The children will assemble at their own school sat 9 the head of the procession leaving the Fairfield schools punctually at 9.20. They will be preceded this year as in days of old, before the Boer War, by the military band from. thq barracks, 20 strong, and will pass the Church-street School at 9.30„ Fennel-street for St. Claorge's at 9.35, and Mersey-street. for St. Clement's at 9.40, reaching the Town Hall at 10.0. There we shall salute the Mayor by whose kindness we assemble on the green and at 10.15 we shall leave by the usual route for the Parish Church. The order of the procession will then be that which was agreed to by the Incumbents last year, viz. : 1, The Parish Church ; 2,St. Peter's; 3, St. Paul's; 4, St!. Ann's ; 5, Holy Trinity; 6, St. Baznabas ; 7, St. James.' A special service will beheld at 11, the preacher being the Rev. Maurice Bell, Vicar of St. Katharine's, Abercromby Square, Liverpool. At 2 by the kindness of the Principal,the Training College field will be thrown open to the scholars till 8, tea be- ing providcd at the various schools at 3, 4 and 5. Before and after, viz., from 2 to 5 and Gto 8 the band will play for dancing on the green.

GRAPPEN HALL.

JUMBLE SALE.—A jumble sale in aid of the Cricket Club was held in the large room on Thursday afternoon from four to seven o'clock. There was a good assort- ment of articles for sale and customers turned up very strongly, with the result that the funds of the Club will be the gainer by about fifteen pounds. THE CRICKET CLUB.—The Ist eleven were unfortu- nate in haying opponents who could not raise a team. Whit Saturday being a holiday time, consequently they were without match. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

DISGRACEFUL STREET SCENES.—BURNING EFFIGIES. At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, Priscilla Hartlana, and Elizabeth Hartland, 21 Bond-street, mother and dAughter were summoned for camraitting a breach of the peace by acting in a disorderly manner in Plumpton-street on May 20th.—The Chief Censtable said the case arose out bf another which was heard by the Bench on Friday week. A commentary upon their worship's decision was to burn the effigy or the person in whose favour they had decided the case. This kind of thing went on regularly in certain neighbourhoods when a case had been heard at the Court. The popular verdict was given by creating almost a riot, burning eTgies, and acting generally in a way that would disgrace heathen savages.—P.C. Reddington said he was on duty in Liverpool-road when he saw a large I crowd collected in Plumpton-street. Mrs. Hartland I was wheeling an effigy about on a bicycle. Witness spent considerable time in trying to clear the crowd away and Elizabeth Hartland then brought out another effigy and commenced to wheel it about the street, at the same time shouting and acting in a disorderly way. Ultimately one of the ecflgies was set on fire, and the neighbourhood was in a state of semi-riot during the whole of the evening.—John Large, 22, Plumpton-street, against whom the demonstration was directed, also gave evidence.—Defendants denied that they were the prime movers in the matter.—The Chief Constable said he had given instructions that in all future cases of e`fiay-burning. the offenders should be at once arrested. He asked the Bench to make an example of defeadants.—The magistrates bound the defendants over to keep the peace,Priscilla Hartland to pay 10s. costs and Elizabeth ss. ALLEGED ASSAULT ON A TRAM CONDUCTOR. Thomas Warburton, Gorsey-lane, was charged at the Warrington Police Courton Wednesday, with assaulting ' Richard Holloway, tram conductor, on May lath.—Mr. Stanley Wilson, deputy town clerk, prosecuted and Mr. Browne defended.—Mr. Wilson stated that the defendant was a passenger on the 11 p.m. car from the Central Station. On arriving at the Buttermarket-street end of Scotland-road, two: men who were considerably under the influence of drink attempted to board the car which was fall of; passengers. The conductor very properly refused to allow them to get on but as the men were persistent, P.C. Gibbs, who happened to be a passenger, cane to his assistance. The men were ant off and the car resumed its journey. Defendant immediately commenced to abuse the conductor, calliag him foul n unes and stating that the men were not drunk and that Holloway had no right to put them off the car. While Holloway was collecting the fares, defendant began to art.tue the point with P.C. Gibbs. The argument was continued until the officer left the car at Salisbury-street. Defendant threatened the con- ductor who warned him several times to desist, and told the defendant that he wrold require his name and address. These the defendant refused to give. When the car reached the terminus, defendant was abant to leave when the c inductor put his hands oa his shoulder and again asked him for his name and adslress. Defendant thereupon turned round and dealt Holloway a severe blow on the side of the face. Leaving the car, defend- ant challenged Holloway to come and fight him. A number of defendant's friends were urging him on to attack the conductor. He (Mr. Wilson) submitted that the conductors held somewhat responsible positions esvcially when the cars were fall, and he asked the Beneh to sannort the co nmittee in taking that action.— Evidence having been given hearing out this statement Mr. Browne contended that the case was a most tru np- ery,and trivial affair Defendaat happened to s tylto a friend that the conductor had'no right to refuse to allow the men to hoard the car as they were not drunk-. He denied using abasive language or striking Holloway. If any person ought to be summoned it was the conductor for inter- fering with the defendant. The case ought never to have been brought into Court —Frank Page, 123 Marsh- halise-lane. Oswald Litton, Padgate, and Mrs. Harvey, with whom the defendant lodges, stated that there was nothing in the nature of an assault. Defendant simply pushed the conductor.— Defendant was further charged with making use of obscena I,naaaaa—Mr. Browne sail this summons had been taken out as an afterthought when it was known that the Case was to he defended.—The Chairman (Alderman Sutton) said the fat nch thought there were faalts on both sides. it woald have been much better if Holloway-had kept his hands off the defendant,but in any case the defendant had on right to interfere with the eonductor. He must pay the costs of the cases (13a) OLIVE STONES FOR PEPPER—A PENKETH PROSECUTION. At the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wed- nesday before Colonel Riclaway and Mr. T. Halton, Peter Heath, grocer, 17 Warrington-road, Penketh, was charged with selling 6oz. of white pepper which as not of the quality and substance demanded by the pur- chaser.—Mr. Wilson prosecuted for Lancashire County Council and Mr. William Rudd, solicitor, Liverpool, defended.—lt will be remembered that at a previous hearing the defendant's solicitor stated that a leading London analyst had certified a sample of the pepper to be pure.—Mr. Rudd applied for an adjournment in order that a portion of the pepper might be sent to the Government laboratories to be analysed.—Mr. Wilson now stated that a certificate had been received entirely corroborating the analysis of the County Analyst. Sergeant Ross, acting on the instructions of Superin- tendent Cropper, went to the defendant's shop and asked for 6oz. of white pepper. He told defendant it was required for analysis. A sample of the pepper was sent to the County Analyst, and it was found to contain 2 percent. of ground olive stones. The County Authorities had had a great deal of trouble with cases of adulter- ation and they were generally traceable to the same firm. Grocers had no right to sell pepper containing olive stones.—Mr. Rudd for the defence said his client now admitted the offence. The pepper was sold in the same state that it was bought from the manufacturer, and defendant had every reason to believe it was pure. —The magistrates imposed a fine of £3 including costs, and an advocate's fee of one guinea was allowed. A CONTEMPTIBLE FRAUD—IMPOSING ON THI CHARITABLY DISPOSED.

Mary Naylor. who said she came from Bolton, plpaded guilty at the Warrington Police Court yesterday to obtaining alms under false pretences.—The Chief Constable said he considered the case of snch imr.lrtance as to ask for a remand for a v ‘ek in order to inquire into the prisoner's Itecedents. She was obviously-getting her living•bv one of the meanest and most contemptible forms of fraud that human ingenuity could conceive* She appeared to have been going about to working people and small shop-keepers and Thursday, and repre- sentine that she was in fearful distress, that she had a baby dead in the house. and she was collecting money for the funeral. He did not know how the people managed to believe that story because the prisoner was carrying a baby in her arms. Sometimes she varied the story by sayine that her husband was dead, and she was absolutely destitute. She managed to impose Upon several charitably-disposed people who gave her money. She actually posted a little boy—who was a perfect straneer to her—in the street to watch for the police. P.C. Clarke came upon the scene and arrested prisoner on the suspicion of Mrs. Cross,lB, Knutsford-road,upon whom she had imposed. Prisoner tried to get Mrs.

Cross to go round the corner, away from the policeman, and she would explain to her. Ultimately she had ad- mitted that the stories she had told to excite com- miseration were nothing but lies and fabrications.— Prisoner was remanded for a week.

In the morning prisoner had decamped, and it was foand that the pockets of several of the men had been rifled and small sums of money taken. Police-constable Bennett was on duty in Horse- market street on Sunday morning and met the prisoner 'who was in civilian clothes. The officer suspected that the man was a soldier and challenged him. Prisoner first denied havin,, been connected with the service in any way. but on being pressed admitted having deserted. that he had committed the robbery.—An officer from, the barracks said the prisoner was a recruit and had been connected with the regiment less than a month.—Prisoner was sentenced to a month's imprisonment for the theft, and on expiration of the term he will be dealt with by the military authorities for desertion.—The constable was recommended for the usual reward. ANOTHER MARKET THEM—Ellen Thompson, of James-street, was charged with stea,ling from the market place a pair of boots, of the value of Bs. 6d., the pro- perty of Thomas Lee—The Chief Constable said the prisoner had, been on remand since Tuesday in order that the owner of the boots might be found. It was now ascertained that prisoner stole the boots from Mr. Lee's stall on Saturday evening, and offered them in pledge on Monday to Mr. Domvi Ile, pawnbroker, Knntsford-road. She gave her name as Mrs. Thompson, 16, Wash-lane, but as Mr. Doraville suspected her, he asked her to whom the boots belonged. Prisoner replied that a Mrs. Marsh, of Wash-lane, had given them to her. Mr. •nyille told the prisoner to go and bring the owner of the boots, and she went away, presumably with that object, but never returned. Mrs. Marsh was seen, but she knew nothing about the boots. Prisoner was arrested by Snnerintendent Monaghan, and when charged re- peated the old old story " I found them on the floor near the stall. I knew -I had no right to take them." It appeared as if market thieves read the papers, and kept clippings of such cases, as the excuse was so often given.—Prisoner, who had been before the Court on three other charge of theft from the market, was sen- tenced to two months' hard labour. Re EDWIN Pl'E.—E.lwin Pye. clothier, outfitter and house furnisher, 103, Longford-street, Warrington, appeared for his public examination and was represented by Mr. Steel.—The debtor's statement of affairs showed liabilities expected to rank at £333 18s., and the assets £B7 6s. id., leaving a deficiency of £246 lls. 11d. He commenced business in February. 1901, without capital, and he attributed his failure to interest on loans, bad trade ; the wet season; insufficient capital ; purchase of stock for which I found there was no demand in the neighbourhood of my shop and household and personal expenses in excess of profits."—The examination was closed. BISCUIT DEALER'S B INKRUPTCY.—Edmund Green Barrett, biscuit and cake merchant, now residing at 63, Hale-street, but previously at 3G, Thewlis-street, and 121, Longford-street and the Market Hall, was publicly examined.—Mr. Steel represented tht. debtor. His statement of affairs showed liabilities amounting to £124 195., with assets £32 6s. 21., showing a deficiency of £92 12s 10d. He stated that he commenced business in February. 1903, with £3B free capital, and carried it on, with a partner, until December, 1903, and subsequently alone. He attributed his failure to law costs; an acci- dent to a van; loss on a horse; loss on stock spoiled in transit, and loss on the purchase of his business.—The examination was closed. Re WILLIAM BECKET'', OF WESTON POINT--William Beckett, grain sampler and timber measurer,Post Office- lane, Weston Point, near Runcorn also appeared for his public examination. MAIN AT CROFT.—Oa Saturday the members of the bowling club attached to the Horse Shoe Inn, held their annnal opening games for prizes. The green was in splendid condition and some exciting contests were witnessed. The successful members were :-1, R. Sanderson; 2, James Taylor; 3, W. Maines ; 4, H. Shaw; 5, A. Pomfret; 6, A. Owen. After bowling, a substantial tea, prepared by host and hostess Maines, was heartily enjoyed. Mr. Shaw presided and distribu- ted the prizes. The officers for the ensuing year were also elected. Mr. Shaw was appointed president, Mr. Oakes vice-president, Mr. J. Maines treasurer, and Mr. H. Hankinson secretary. The retiring committee were also re-elected.

THE STRANGE TRICKS IT DOES.

At first this seems like a strange thing ; almost an MY absurd thing. Can you account for it ? " case puzzled me,' says Mrs. Martha Beverley, "and puzzled others. I had a good appetite and ate plenty of nourish- ing food, yet I lost flesh and got weaker all the time." The lady says she had suffered from indigestion and debility for twenty years. What do we mean by debility? Is it a disease ? No, it is an effect of a disease. "Food did not strengthen me," says Mrs. Beverley, "but often gave me much pain across the chest and between the shoulders. Often, when I stooped down, I could hardly straighten myself up again. As the years rolled by I got worse, and was always in pain after eating. My body would swell with wind and I had fearful gripmg in the stomach. Sometimes they made me faint dead away. For months I knew not what it was to have a good night's sleep. My breath was short and I was troubled with palpitation of the heart. To live was to carry a load almost greater than I could bear. I was pale as a ghost and was scarcely more than bones covered with skin. Now, however, thanks to Mother Seigel's Syrup, I am well and strong again. "A friend (who was a friend indeed) argued its merits to me until I gave up and tried it. Mother Seigel's Syrup first cured my indigestion and all the pains, aches and ailments that were caused by it. Then it made me profit by the food I ate, so I gained flesh fast ; and with that came new life and strength. I used Mother Seigel's Syrup regularly for six months and it delivered me completely from a desease that had clung to me for twenty years, and came near laying me in the grave. What I would say to sufferers is this :—Use Mother Seigel's Syrup and keep on using it; don't doubt or get discouraged. Where one takes it now a thousand should take it. When she made that statement on July 18th, 1900— four years ago—Mrs. Beverley lived at 102, Lumley- I street, Middlesboro'. On February 18, 1904—this year— 1 at her new home, 154, Abingdon-road, Middlesboro', she made and signed this statement: "Referring to my testimonial of four years ago, to the merits of Mother Seigel's Syrup, I am pleased to inform you that I have enjoyed good health ever since. I still take a dose occasionally on the principle that prevention is better than cure; but the old complaint never troubles me now. For twenty years I had endured the tortures of indigestion when Mother Seigel's Syrup restored me to health. From little beginnings it had come to be the haunting menace of my life. My doctor declared I should never recover, but he was wrong. Mother Seigel's Syrup restored me to perfect health, and I feel that I can never praise it too highly." Indigestion does more tricks than any other necro- mancer. Watch it and use the Syrup immediately.

THE VOICE AND THE PHONOGRAPH.

- _ To prove that a man's voice does not sound the same to himself as to others. Dr. L. Laloy causes several per- sons to speak into a phonograph, and after a few days reproduces the sentencos. Each person recognises the voices of his friends,but not his own. Professor Exner explains that passage through solids augments the in- tensity of sound and modifies its quality,and the sound of one's own voice is given different timbre by reaching the ear through the air and the bones of the face instead of through the air alone.

The King and Princess of the Shan States in Upper Burma have placed orders Nvith their agents in Rangoon for a gentleman's and a lady's " Premier" Tricycle.

WARRINGTON.

Personal. The Rev. Richard Heber Bowen, of St. Aidan's Theological College, Birkenhead, has been licensed to a curacy at St. Paul's Church, Warrington. Police-sergeant Washington of Lymm has been awarded the first class merit badge for long and meritorious service. The sergeant has been at Lymm a little over two years, but altogether he has been in the force for 22 years. Acting-Sergeant Sharpe, who has been promoted to full sergeant entered upon his pew duties at Bowdon on Wednesday. It was origin- ally intended that he should be drafted to Sale, but that order was countermanded.

Mr. Herbert Parkinson, secondary clerk on the staff of the Warrington Education Committee, has been appointed assistant in the Higher Education Depart- ment of the Surrey County Education Committee at a salary of £l2O. Mr. Parkinson is 25 years of age, and has been over ten years in the service of the Warrington Technical Instruction Committee and the Education Committee. He has been selected from a large number of candidates. His many Warrington friends will wish him success in his new appointment. Prospects of the Cheshire Fruit Crops.

The- warmer weather of the past week

and the

accompanying rain have, effected a marvellous im- provement in crop prospects generally in Cheshire. Not for many years has there been a better show of apple and pear blossom,and the yield of fruit promises to be a splendid one. The raspberry, gooseberry, and currant trees are full of fruit, which is ripening ripidly. The outlook with regard to stone fruit is, however, less favourable. Although damsons and plums bloomed freely only a medium crop is expected. On the Dee banks at Farndon and across the border in Holt an immense area ls under strawberry cultiva- tion. It is early to speak with any degree of certainty but there are encouraging indications of a good crop With the exception of a slight frost on the 20th ult. the weather has been very favourable for the early potatoes, and there is every promise of good hay and corn harvests.

PUBLIC BENEFIT, UNDER the CLOCK for BEST BOCT .1 in LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Lord Cross's Birthday. Among those who attended the King's levee at St. James's Palace on "Monday was Viscount Cross, who yesterday attained the age of eighty-one. The vener- able peer is the son of the late Mr. William Cross, of Red Scar, Preston. Educated at Rugby and Cam- bridge, he was called to the bar in 1849, and sat in the House of Commons for Preston from 1857 to 1862. In the latter year he disappeared tempo- rarily from political life, on which he had made so far no particular impression.— In 1868, however, he saw an opportunity for a grand political coup, and laid the foundation of his future fortunes by beating Mr. Gladstone in South-Wet Lancashire. " Liverpool went crazy for joy," said one contemporaneous writer, and as for Lord Cross's party—it was ready to give him the moon had he asked it. He was first in office as Home Sectetary in 1874 under Mr. Disraeli—for which post his solid merits and sound business capacity fully fitted him. Lord Cross was an intimate friend of her late Majesty Queen Victoria, whom he is believed to have advised constantly and ably in her private business. FOR CLASS AND CiuNA. of every description try

Say for current rate £lOO gross and £B5 rateable. and then to be re-valued.—The report was adopted.

The quarterly session was held on Saturday at Southport, and there was an attendance of over a hundred including representatives from Warrington, Leigh, Widnes, &c. The District Chief Templar, Rev. J. A. Lee, in his report, noted the large amount of interest shown throughout the dis- trict. The numerical position, as defined by Bro. Morris Jones, District Secretary, was debated at some length, while the D.S.J.T. Bro. Newman (Earlestown) gave an account of the juvenile department, a large accession to the mem- bership having been made. The district executive dealt with a number of matters, including the recent decease of Bro. R. W. Kirkus, P.D.C.T , who had been an active official for over a quarter of a century. District Treasurer Bro. G. B. Walker initi- ated a.discussion on finances, which resulted in the adoption of a resolution whereby the income will be considerably increased. Bro. It. W. Rycroft, D.E.S., subjected the Licensing Bill to a scathing criticism, and it was decided to authorise the D.C.T., to sign a petition to the House of Commons, strongly urginn. the rejection of the bill. It was also arranged Co hold a demonstration on June 19. The next mectincr is to be held at St. Helens.

Proposed Alterations in the Surveyor's

Duties.

This authority held its fortnightly meeting at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday under the presidency of Mr. S. Woodward. There were also present :—Messrs. 61. Pilling (vice-chairman), C. E. Linaker, T. Batter, C. B- Linaker, jun., J. Mounfield, T. Wright, J. L. Jones, Whitley, A. A. G. Guest, J. Bolshaw, J. Whitlow, a Roylance, W. Fletcher, J. Hallows, J. T. Collier, T.• Antwis, H. Eyes, P. Mounfleld, A. Broome, H. M. Davies, J. Smith, H. MawdsleY, Harrison, S. Horton, E. Bretherton, R. s'• Chrimes, J. Spruce, R. Billington, Admiral Jones Para, with Dr. Adams (medical officer of health), Messrs. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (deputy clerk), J. Far- rington (inspector of nusiances), J. _ MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT. Dr. Adams reported that a case of diphtheria at Stockton Heath,a case of scarlet fever at StocktonHeathl and another at Grappenhall, and a case of typhoid fever at Five Crosses, Frodsham, had been notified. The Frodsham Lordship Parozhial Committee were .asked to inquire into the sanitary arrangements in the last named case. PRECAUTIONS AGAINST MOTOR ACCIDENTS. A circular letter was received from the Cheshire County Council pointing out that by the Motor Car Act an obligation was placed upon local authorities to set up sign posts denoting dangerous corners, cross roads and precipitous places. The County Council's Association and Municipal Corporations' Ass(' elation had proposed uniform symbols for the purpose, and as the Local Government Bear' considered that they might conveniently be adopted they did not propose to issue regulations upon the sub- ject. The Main Roads and Bridges Committee of the County Council had appointed a sub-committee to con- sider and report as to the erection of sign posts, notice &c., upon the highways in the County, and the Rural Council was invite.; to make suggestions as to where in their opinion there was any real necessity for such warnings. As the cost of erecting and maintaining these posts would be considerable, the advisability was sug- gested of local authorities endeavouring as far ao possible to prevail upon owners and occupiers to keep fences at cross roads as low as possible so as not to obstruct the view of persons using the road. The Clerk said he had forwarded copies of the letter to the surveyors with a request that they would report to the Council.

Mr. Baxter thought the matter was quite outside the business of the Council. The motorists had an Automo- bile Club and they ought to erect the sign-posts. /le objected to the spending of a single shilling towarde making it easy for these men to travel and run anyone down.

The Chairman: The Act of 1003 places an obligation upon the local authority to provide the signs. Mr. Baxter: Let them pick their own dangerous places. I propose that we take no notice of the letter. The Chairman: I am afraid we shall have to de something. The County Council pay the whole of the expense.

Mr. Baxter: It comes out of our pockets all the same.

Mr. Boishaw seconded Mr. Baxter's proposition, but on a vote being taken it was defeated. The Surveyors were instructed to prepare reports oil the matter.

APPLETON SEWER EXTENSION. With regard to the question of extending the sewer in Birchdale-lane, Appleton, Mr. Farrington, inspector, reported that it would necessitate an expenditure of something like £4O.

It vras decided to obtain tendern for the work. PILLAIOSS-LANE, WALTON. Messrs. White, MacTver and Co., Warrington, wrote under date May 25th. with regard to the taking over of Pillmoes-lane, stating :—"Tf the Council will take the road over from to-day and at once put it into propf?r condition and maintain the same as a public road, Sir Gilbert Greenall will give the stories which he Pur- chased from Messrs. Fowles, Fairhurst, and Mounflekl, and also on completion of the work will pay gloo towards the cost of the same."

The Council approved the terms. THE SURVEYOR'S DUTIES.-PROPOSED A LTERATIOIkIS% Mr. Linaker said it would be within the recollection of some members of the Council that originally the , duties of the surveyors were to attend to the highwaYe pure and simple. Additional duties were thrown upon them at a time when it was thought that by making them assistant inspectors of nuisances the Council would recover from the Local Government Board a portion of their salaries. The work of attending to the sewers, which formerly devolved upon Mr. Farrington, was put upon the surveyors. It was never alleged that Mr. Farrington was overburdened with duties. They knew he did his work in a very excellent manner, but he had'never complained that he had more than he could do. Siace that time the surveyors had not only hod charge of the highways and sewers, but they had also at times been engaged in preparing plans for additional sewers in various townships. There had been no saving of expense in this direction, Wells?, the surveyors had in many cases been paid a commissie': on the work done. The surveyors had also had to PII" upon them the passing of the building plat under thei, Building Bye-laws, the inspection of the houses, ane reporting. He certainly felt the Council had put oP°ll the shoulders of the surveyors more work than should have been put upon them, if they were to giv,e the time necessary to the performance of be' duties. There were 162.3- miles of road in the, district, and the surveyors had to do a great deal of travelling. He believed that if the duties were re-arranged, the Council would get better results from the expenditure on salaries. During the year ending September 1902 the expenditure on the, , highways was £4,548; during the year ending Michcelmas 1904 the expenditure was £6,609. That was equal to about 45 per cent increase. He moved the appointment of the following committee to report on the matter-- Messrs. Baxter, Wright, Woodward, Pilliag, Bolshaws Antwis, Broome, Mounfield, and Linaker. Admiral Jones Parry seconded. It was ultimately decided that • the whole Council should consider the matter at a special meeting.

CHAT.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

Lace is very much worn this -season, ane .uckv possessors of heirlooms in the form ol old lace scarves and shawls will find abun- dant scope for exercising their artistic talents in the draping of these treasures with sof+ folds which will glorify summer dresses, blouses, or even coats, and increase their value in every way. Both black and white lace are greatly in demand, and in some instances the two are even to be met with in striking contrast on one and the same toilette. In black Chantilly is perhaps the favourite make, and it shows to great advan- tage when draped over white. Cream ane yellowish lace harmonises, of course, with any material and any colour, but it is observable that the tendency this season is towards employing the finer makes of lace in pre- ference to the coarser varieties which for one or two summers have been so markedly popular. This leaning towards fine laces probably arises from the fact that all material'

A SUMMER SKIRT.

are of the diaphonous, delicate kind, and are not laid flat on the lining, whether of bodice or skirt, but are gauged or tucked whenever possfble. Let us hope that the weather during the next three months will offer numerous opportunities for wearing these airy-fairy toilettes which demand unclouded skies and brilliant sunshine.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
My first sketch gives a good sample of the elaborate skirt for summer wear such as I have referred to. It is in this instance f biscuit-coloured voile gauged round the hips, and pleated down to the bands of embroidery headed with a narrow gauging of the material at either edge. These bands of embroidery divide the skirt twice, the lower one finishing 'off in a full frill which flows round the feet. A white muslin would make up very charm- ingly in this style, with open muslin em- broidery arranged in a similar fashion; while, if desired, the skirt could be made shorter with less of the trained effect. Indeed, the so-called crinoline skirt promises to reign supreme this season. It is so described, I fancy, because that the fulness of its style which goes on in,:reasing towards the feet produces something not unlike the• old- fashioned crinoline when first introduced and before it develod into the monstrosity whose ugliness has been so faithfully depicted in the pages of "Punch" of that period. Let us hope that the rulers of fashion will not lead

A ROSE-TRIMMED HAT,

ets so far astray as to permit of a revival ot such an exceedingly inartistic and uncom- fortable article of dress. The present style, of dress has most emphatically the effect of reducing the apparent size of the waist by reason of the fulness which springs out above and beiow, and there are some very charm- ing belts of a soft kind which cling closely and add to this desirable result. _ . .

Millinery is charming beyond description i this year, and the pretty hat which s illus- trated forms no mean example of a dainty up-to-date chapeau. It is formed of a burnt shade of fine straw, with the edge of the brim bound with rose-pink velvet, while a wreath of roses are arranged round the crown and form the sole trimming save for a band of gold-pink silk, which passes over the brim at the left side and forms a soft chou at the bandeau at .that side. It is a simple but lovely hat with its beautiful lines and curves. Many girls •find that their last season's hat will again do duty this year, for it had not many opportunities of losing any of its freshness owing to the lack of bright days. A little alteration may be necessary, but even if the hat is slightly soiled its defectS can be concealed by one of the fashionable veils draped cunningly round the crown and forming a curtain at the back. Some of the newest hats are trimmed only by a lace veil and one or two clusters of flowers. Occasion-

We can, then, safely say that the abandon- ment of preference to the Colonies by Great Britain has certainly not brought any evil effect upon British or Colonial trade. Modern Preference.

MR. CHAMBERLAIN imagines that preference to-day will accomplish something that it did not secure fifty years ago. He also wishes us to believe that the Colonies are giving a real preference to British trade and that they are prepared to extend that preference; bit only on condition that we are ready to do the same and to do it at once. Says Mr. Chamberlain:

Believe me, now is the appointed hour. Those rising peoples—still comparatively in their infancy, but making progress which in a few brief years will place them immeasurably beyond their present position—those rising generations cannot wait, they have their destinies to control. They have their paths of progress to mark out. Do you think they will wait for ever on the out- skirts of your indisposition ? No, grasp the occasion while it arises, or it will for evcr slip from your fingers. Now before we grasp anything let us be sure it will not sting us. Let us know what we are grasping. We are asked to abandon cur present Fiscal policy in order to give a prefer- ence to the Colonies, not- merely for the sake of the Empire, but also for the sake of our trade!

If Mr. Chamberlain will drop the idea of benefiting British trade by his scheme, it would greatly simplify matters, and we could then look at his proposals purely from the patriotic point of view. But as he will insist upon parading his proposals as tie only means of improving British trade we are bound to judge them from a purely commercial stand- point. --- Business Questions.

- Brushing aside all Mr. Chamberlain's high- sounding words, business men will want an answer to this question: "Has the preference given by tie Colonies increased British trade to a larger extent than foreign trade ? " The fipures give the reply. Here are the values of British and American exports to Canada:

Year. Total. British. Americau. 1897 21,323,000 5,880,000 11,404,000 1893 25,261,000 6,406,000 14,800,0C° 1899 29,870,000 7,356,000 17,700,000 1900 34,501,000 8,856,000 20,416,000 1901 35,500,000 8,563,000 21,420,000 1902 39,295,000 r,801,0n0 22,950,000 1903 44,962,000 11,758,000 25,758,010

These figures show, that the preference given to British trade by Canada is not, in practice, equal to that given to the United States of America.

The second question business men will want answered is: "Will the promised preference increase British trade more than foreign trade ? " The reply to that question is that so far as we have been able to discover no promise of further preference has ever been made by anybody on behalf of the Colonies, except by Mr. Chamberlain himself! Now Mr. Chamberlain may be a very great man, but he -has no mandate to speak for the Colonies; that being the case, would any business man speculate a brass button upon any political promise Mr. Chamberlain miht make? The only preference the Colonies could give which would be worth while to con- sider would be an equal market for British manufactures, but this the Colonial manufac- turers'have refused to give. They are quite willing to give us a sort of preference, that is, they are prepared to increase their duties against foreign goods. But they will not accept any alteration of their tariffs which does not give an adequate protection for their own industries against those of Great Britain. It is for such a flimsy advantage that Mr. Chamberlain asks us to tax our food and endanger our foreign trade.

The fine hitting feat of Gilbert Jessop when he scored 28 in one over may rank as a record for county cricket, but it is a long way below what has been accomplished in club games. Only the other day K. E. M. Barker, of Surrey and the Wanderers, hit one of the Cranbrook trundlers for 28 in an over, and this is by no means an r ncommon performance, it having been done .a several occasions in the London district. It is only about two years since when the Bath cricketer (W. Hyman) indulged in a truly cyclonic innings. In one hour and fifty minutes the Western county m'an actually scored olfhis own bat 359 out of 466. In two overs W. Hyman hit 64 runs, or 32 an over. It was against E. M. Grace's lobs too, and as 466 runs were registered in 110 minutes one can well appreciate the extent of such hurricane hitting. There is another instance where a club man hit four sixes and a seven in one over against very fast bowling. Hitters in club cricket are very common. Many years ago a Mitcham man is said to have hit a ball on the old green for twelve, and then expired, having ruptured a blood vessel

A PRETTY BLOUSE.

ally two veils are to be seen, the one worn permanently down the back, and the other for wearing over the face when desired. The latter is generally of a very fine make of net with a lace border.

Blouses arc, of course, in great demand, and a very pretty One is given in my last sketch, made with bands of silk thread open- work as a trimming, finished by pretty little silk tassels. A thin woollen material or a pretty transparent voile would make up nicely in this style over a silk under-bodice. In all bodices or blouses the long-shouldered yoke effect is worn, and is expressed in many fashions, sometimes by bands as in this blouse, and at 'other times by a yoke and shoulder-piece in one of lace or embroidery. The fichu effect or the simulated bolero are two fashionable modes which I can recom- mend, each being suited to different kinds of figures. It is noticeable, too, that the fulness of the fashionable sleeves no longer continues into a large pouf above the wrist, but stops short just after the turn of the elbow. Some bodices show a sleeve that ends there, neces- sitating the wearing of long suede gloves, while others again have the arm covered down to the wrists by a long cuff of lace or embroidery. The latter, I fancy, wilt be ;owl(' the most popular and useful styled . COQUETTIiI

MR. CHAMBERLAIN'S UNAUTHORISED PROGRAMME.

OLD RAGS AND WORN-OUT PANS.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADE AND _EMPIRE IN DANGER.

WHEN Mr. Chamberlain re-stated his case at Birmingham on May 12th, he gave us no new light upon his proposals of Preference and Protection. Beyond the fact that he now describes them as his second "unauthorised programme" there was scarcely a fresh sen- tence in the speech. To adopt his own phrase we had nothing but

"the waving of old flags and the clatter- ing of worn-out pans." The spiech at Birmingham was the same old cry: Your Empire and Trade is in danger.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
Ancient Preference.

ME. CHAMBERLAIN sometimes speaks as though he imagined that the idea of Prefer- ence for Colonial produce was something entirely new. If he entertains any hope of securing the patent rights for that proposal, a study of the records will quickly shew him that the plan was tried in the first half of last century and abandoned as useless. If we take the figures of exports to British Colonies before and after those Colonies enjoyed preferential treatment in our markets, we shall find some interesting results:

BRITISH EXPORTS TO COLONIES UNDER PREFERENTIAL TARIFFS.

1839 16,345,769 1844 16,712,712 1840 17,458,307 1845 17,077.060 1841 15,003,425 1846 16,165,315 1842 13,473,064 1E47 15,147,679 1843 15,228,834 1848 13,035,543 It will be seen that during the time we were giving a preference to the Colonies our trade was small and unsteady; it fluctuated back- ward and forward in a most unsatisfactory manner. Now let us consider the figures after preference was abandoned. For brevity we will give the average for each five years:

BRITISH EXPORTS TO COLONIES UNDER FREE TRADE. E E 1859 37,000,000 1890-4 78,000,000 1860-4 46,000,000 1895-9 81,000,000 1865-9 50,000,000 1900 94,000,000 1870-4 60.00 D 000 1901 104,000,000 1875-9 67,000,000 1902 109,000,000 1880-4 81,000,080 1903 111,000,000 1885-9 79,000,000

These figures are eminently satisfactory. If we turn to our imports from. the Colonies we find the same steady upward progress. In spite of the fact that the Colonies have now no preference. in the British market, and have to enter upon the same terms as foreign nations, still our Colonies have been more than able to hold their own.

IMPORTS FROM THE COLONIES TO GREAT BRITAIN. Z L 1855-9 40,000,000 1890-4 96,000,000 1860.4 68,000,000 1895-9 98,001,00 1E65-9 68,000,000 1900 109,000,000 1870-4 76,003,000 1901 105.000,000 1875-9 83,000,000 3902 106,000,000 1280-4 96,000,000 1903 113,000,000 1E25-9 87,000,000

STOCKTON HEATH.

THE SEWAGE QUESTION AT LOWER WALTON.—As Mr. F. Monks very naturally objected to the sewage running into the brook within his grountois he lodged a complaint with the Runcorn Rural Sanitaty Authorities. The latter appointed a special committee to investigate into the alleged nuisance, and as a result we understand it is advised that Stockton Heath should be approaced with the view of making an arrangement for the purpose of allowing the sewage to be carried into their sewers.

THE MAIN SEWERS.—INTENDING APPLICATIO'NFROM LOWER WAETON.—We learn that it is contemplated by the authntities of Lower Walton to approach the Stockton Heath Authority for permission to be allowed to enter their main sewer for the purpose of carrying away some of the sewage of Lower Walton.—As) it is considered that the Stockton Heath sewers are in danger of being congested owing to the rapid increase of the district it is doubtful whether consent will be given. In Stockton Heath itself it is felt that it should now face a new sewage scheme and it is probably only a lack of public spirit or courage about the expense that is keeping the question back.

In the House of Commons on Tuesday Mr. Winston Churchill left his former place amongst the Unionist rank and file, and took a seat on the front bench below the gangway on the Opposition side.
