LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

The National Service League and the Report of the Royal Commission. Sirt—the momentous report of the Royal Commis- sion on the auxiliary forces presided over thighe Duke of Norfolk will inevitably excite much die ion, and justifies the National Service League in asking for public support. The League was established in February 1902 for the express purpose of continuously urging the necessity of general military or naval training for home defence. Now that its objects have been endorsed by a Royal Commission, the League, though in no way pledged to the detailed proposals of the report, may claim to be a rallying point for all who are in accord with its main pt inciple. The National Service League, which counts a con- siderable number of militia and volunteer officers among its members, is fully alive to the sacrifices that have been and are being made by the auxiliary forces. But the League believes that it is unfair and impossible to ask these forces to continue by their exclusive efforts to relieve the rest of the civilian population from their responsibilities. The higher standard of training and discipline now required from them cannot be obtained except bY farther and larger sacrifices. Is there any reasonable expectation that such sacrifices will ba forthcoming on the part of a sufficient number of physically qualified men? The majority of the Royal Commissioners after a searching investigation into the efficiency and numbers of the auxiliary forces confirms these views. In the minority report (there is no pain like that of a new idea) which shows most reluctance to accept the principle of compulsion, far more stringent training and discipline for the auxiliary forces are advocated. But even Sir R. Knox is constrained to allow that the "requisite number of young men of the necessary physique may not be forthcoming voluntarily," and admits that if this condition is not fulfilled "the only way of meeting the difficulty" would be "to apply some system of compulsion." There are other arguments in favour of some form of general military or naval training beyond those adduced by the Commissioners, who were necessarily limited to a certain aspect of the question. They are not of a purely military character. The conventional view that economic loss is involved in the withdrawal from pro- duction for a short p3riod of military training of a certain number of the population is apparently undergoing revision. At any rate such a view is emphatically repudiated by our neighbours, who have had actual experience of compulsory service and whose economic progress is undoubted. Closely allied with the question of economic efficiency is that of physical fitness. The marked inprovernent in physique recorded among certain continental peoples is distinctly ascribed to military training. Again the air is now full of imperial sentiment and appeals. But is there anything which will bring home so vividly to the population of these islands the consciousness of empire and the responsibilities awl duties it entails as the devotion of time and personal exertion necessary to qualify them for its defence? The National Service League therefore confidently invites the support of all who realise the importance of general training to co-operate in keeping this question before the public mind. •Much has been done and is being done for the promotion of Cadet Corps, Rifle Clubs, Miniature Rifle Shooting,&c. Everything accom- plished in these directions will tend to make the burden of subsequent training more natural and less onerous, and to procure better results from its adoption. But the value of these efforts would be immensely enhanced if they were associated with the main principle which the National Service League was organised to promote. —We have the honour to be, your obedient servants, WELLINGTON, HARDINGE, • NEWTON, J. WOLFE BARRY, HENRY J. BIRCEIENOUGH, N. BOWDEN-SMITE!, C. E. DAWKINS, C. S. GOLDMANN, W. F. S. MANN, E. P. WARREN, ROBERT YERBURGH, 31st May, 1904. The National Service League, Dacre House, Victoria-street, S.W.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

Warburton Bridge Tolls. A PENKETH CYCLING ACCIDENT.

The monthly meeting of this Council was held at. Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday, Mr. T. Halton presid- ing. There were also .present :—Dr. Murray, Messrs. Brooks, Ball, Mather, Thompson, Knowles, Forshaw, with Dr. Sephton (medical officer of health), Messrs. J C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), F. Ker- foot (inspector of nuisances), B. S. Newcombe(surveyor) and H. H. Burnage (assistant surveyor).

RETURNS OF FIRES WANTED.

A letter WAS read from the Rome 01Ice stating that

the -.Select Committee appointed by the HOU'3.B of Commons in 1900 to inquire into the subject of the pro- vision of fire brigades recommended that returns should be obtained annually by a Government Department from all local authorities of the number or fires occurring, the loss in life and property, and the number of fire brigades. The Secretary of State had decided that a return for 1903 should be presented to Parliament, and the Council were asked to supply infprmation relating to their own district. Mr. BROOKS : Would it not be best to refer the letter to the Warrington Fire Brigade ? We have no statistics. The CLERK : There is no fire brigade within the dis- trict. I think it would be useful if inspector would make notes of all fires occurring in the district. It was pointed out that no obligation rested upon the Warrington brigade to attend fires in the rural district. The Clerk said he had not yet received a reply to his inquiry as to the scale of charges proposed to be made upon rural authorities to retain the services of the brigade. Mr. NIAMIER : The brigade have never refused to render services, but they can refuse. In the course of further discussion, the CLERK expressed the opinion that Burtonwood ought to have an arrangement with a fire brigade—from Warrington, Newton, St. Helens—on their own account. Mr. FORSHAW said the Parish Council had had under cOnsideration tho provision of appliances to obtain water from the main in ease of fire. It was pointed Out tiPt lir W 44 not aUllcient pressitfA tom the maing. The matter then dropped. FgARNREAD SEWA CIE.—T.IIg i..*GGESTED ARRANGEMENT The CLERK read a letter from the clerk to the Poulton Parish council stating that that authority bad met the medical officer of health, the surveyor, and inapector,with reference to the Fearnhead sewerage ques- tion. Mr. George Mather, the District Council repre- sentative, was also in attendance. Subsequently the Council met and unanimously resolved:— That this Council having very carefully considered the question of the sewerage of Fearnhead, suggest that the District Council call upon the owner or owners of the "hoases in Orchard street and Fearnhead lane to construct •a settling tank or tanks to receive the sewage from the property, as suggested by Dr. Senhton, such tanks to ba emptied periodically, this Council being of opinion that if this be carried out the objections of Dr. Sephton will be met, and thereby an expensive selvage scheme avoided." Dr. SEPHTON said the conference was the outcome of his report to the Local Government Board for two years that he wished to have a better system of drainage if possible at Fearnhead. The matter came before the Council some years ago and plans were prepared of a very costly scheme which was abandoned. He was quite willing that the arrangement mentioned in the resolution should pass for the time being. The CLERK pointed out that if the property owners did not comply with the request the Council would have to undertake a very expensive scheme. Dr. SEPEITON said it would be necessary to obtain the permission of the owners to place the tank on their land. The matter was referred to the o'licials to report further upon.

A PENNETII CYCLING ACCIDENT. CLAIM FOR DAMAGES.

Mr. H. P. C. WRITE, of Halton View Penketh, wrote under date May 17th, that his wife had a bad fall from her bicycle the previous night in Station-road, Penketh, near the Co-operative Stores,owing to the way in which the road had been left by men who had apparently been laying a pipe across it. The doctor said she would be laid up for ten days or a fortnight, and he claimed damages on her behalf from the District Council, in the sum of £5. He might say th tt the roads about there were frequently left in a rough, unfinished state, by the men doing the kind of work mentioned. Mr. KNOWLES : Was it our men who did the work? The St7RVEYOR : No, sir. Mr. THOMPSON : Who did the work? l'he SURVEYOR : A local plumber, I believe. We are not liable. The work was done without the permis- sion or knowledge of the Council. The CLERK : Our answer is that the Council has no liability in the matter. 'The CHAIRMAN remarked that no plumbers or anyone else who wanted to take up the road should do so with- out giving notice to the surveyor. It was wrong that they should be allowed to do so.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
A POINT OF LTA.BILITV.

The CLERK presented a bill from the Corporation in respect of the treatment of a patient who was kept in the Infections Diseases Hospital 59 days. He remarked that that was an exceptionallylona time forsuch a case. Dr. SEPHTON pointed out that complications arose. The CLERK said he thought the Council was liable only in regard to infectious disease, and if patients had to be detained in consequence of other diseases they should be sent to the Workhouse hospital. Dr. SEPHTON : They have no right to detain them in the isolation hospital after fear of infection has eassed away.

There was no further comment on the matter. TRE SURVEYOR COMPLIMENTED.

Mr MA.TEI-ER- -complimented the Surveyor and his staff upon the expeditious way in which the extensive improvements in Cinnamon-lane, Padgate, had been carried out. The work had been done very quickly and well, and had been favourably commented upon in the parish. HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT.

Dr. SEP RTON reported that during the month there had been 20 births and 34 deaths. The death rate was equal to $5.8 per 1,000 against 30•8 the average for the past three months, and 42 for the corresponding period last year. Twenty-three deaths occurred in Winwick Asylum, and exclusive of these the death rate for the district was 9.3. LONGFORD DEPOT IMPROVEMENTS.

.4.1,••••, ••. The INSPECTOR reported that he visited Longford depot on Monday, and found there had been a great im- provement. A new boiler for supplying motive power had been at work since the previous Wednesday. The manure was being removed direct from the dryers, in- stead of being discharged on the floor. A reservoir for storing water was being constructed and would soon be completed. Mr. IiZtOWLES remarked that the wind had been blow- ing from the east lately, and the smell was not so bad. A week or two ago, when the wind was blowing the other way, there was an " awful smell." The INSPECTOR said the improvement which had been effected was wonderful. Whether it would continue or not, he could not tell. When the work had been com- pleted the Medical Oncer of Health would inspect it. WARBURTON BRIDGE TOLLS.

The SURVEYOR reported that he had interviewed the surveyors to the Bucklow and Irlam Councils with respect to Warburton Bridge tolls. The clerk to the Irlam Urban District Council wrote stating that the matter had been considered by the General Purposes Committee. The Council were very much in favour of Mr. Newcombe's proposals, believing that the freeing of Warburton Bridge from tolls would be a step in the right direction. The Council would be prepared to give all reasonable support to his pro msals, apart from incurring any financial liability, which they would not feel justified in doing, seeing that the bridge was out- side their boundary. If the Warrington Council thought the appointment of a joint deputation to dismiss the proposals with the Ship Canal representative wouJd be advisable, the Bucklow Council would acquiesce, andhe would be pleased to hear what further steps were taken in the matter. Would it not be advisable to solicit the aid of the two County Councils? The SURVEYOR explained that the County Councils had been approached and asked to take over the bridge as a main road, but they declined to do so. If the tolls were abolished the Lancashire County Council would be prepared to take over the bridge as a secondary road. The only matter to decide was how much the Ship Canal Company would take to release the toll gate. The Council deferred further action, pending the reply of the Bucklow Council.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNTY COUNCIL GRANTS.

The SURVEYOR presented the following detailed report on County Council grants :—At your last meet- ing. I was instructed to explain fully the County offers, which are as follow :—The application for the formal increase of main roads maintenance was not acceded to, but the £2OO extra macadam grant will be made per- manent, releasing an equal amount of maintenance, thus increasing the total to £1,796. The application for pay- ment of supervision, etc., for county improvements, was also refused, there being no similar payments to any other district within the county, but the salaries allow- ance was increased from £BO to £lOO per annum, to be included in the £1,796 expenditure. On being remon- strated with for ignoring the improvement estimates sent, in, an additional amount was included in the offer of £7O for kerbing etc. at Sankey Schools. On pointing out to Mr. Schofield that Halton's Bridge improvement was derived from loan monies, and was not an ordinary improvement, he allowed £5O super- vision plans, &c., which will be charged when the work is finished and the account sent in. The county offer for next year now stands as follows :—Ordinary maintenance with extra Macadam, £1,796 ; Re-construction of Man- chester road, £200; kerbing at Sankey school,£7o ;total £2,066;a150 Halton's Bridge payment approximately£l.loo In another visit to the County officials, I urged with regard to Alder Root-lane, Winwick, that we always had the through traffic which should be paidlfor as secondary whether the Vulcan length was adopted or not, and I have now been informed that this road will be scheduled as secondary, and would also be paid for as from the beginning of last year. The permanent increased paymenta from the County will now be as follows :—Maintenance, main roads, £200; salaries, £2O; secondary roads, £36 10s. Some time ago, I applied to Mr. Compton Hall, amongst other things, for a new spittle bridge. Mr. Hall has now surveyed the land i around and s preparing a scheme, and I have good reason to believe that the work will be hurried on with all speed. I have not yet had official intimation of the secondary specials of £5O for Cinnamon-lane, and £5O for Hood-lane, and I think it advisable to sae Mr. Schofield on the matter.

Mr.SitooKs complimented th 9 Snrveyor upon hi q very concise and plain statement and the good terms he had obtained.

SAKE V BROOK.

The SURVEYOR reported that he had ni3t Messrs. Crosfield's representative on the site of their tip on Sankey Brook. and he promised that he would have the tip stripped back and a retaining wall of some kind built. When this was done he would again clean out the brook at that point. lie (Mr. Nexcombe) had also met the Ecclesiastical Commissioners' surveyor at Long- ford,and he was going into the matter of obstructions and sittings in the Sankey Brook. He intended to take the matter up thoroughly and would try to arrange combined action of the different landowners for the purpose of preventing further hoods.

IN AND AROUND WARRINGTON.

The Social Problem.

[BY TIIE FREE CRITIC.]

The public conference held last Sunday evening at Parr Hall on the Social Problem was,so far as Warring- ton is concerned an epoch. No doubt in certain pro- fessed and probably well intentioned religious circles there were shaking of heads and remarks about" The Social Problem " not being a fit and proper. subject for discussion on a Sunday, overlooking the fact that the Founder of Christianity ace.trding to the records of holy writ by precept and example never hesitated on the Sabbath Day to discuss social questions and also to use that day for the betterment of his fellow men. For example lie healed on the Sabbath Day and who does not remember the precept He gave . about the ass falling in the pit on the Sabbath Day. Who fails to see the truth from this example and this teaching? The truth is that the Light is only seen and comprehended as through a glass darkly by many people and it takes a vast amount of hard and strenu- ous living before some are wakened out of their false conceptions of life and Christianity. Let me'say before I mention Mr.Dunnico's attitude in arranging for the public conference, that I am neither a!member of his church or his congregation and there- fore cannot be accused of being swayed by partiality in the matter. Now what with regard to Mr. Dunnicocs attitude ? I think in arranging it, he did a courage- ous and noble act—courageous in the first instance, because he had to run counter against the ideas of many good people, and in the second instance—noble, because he invited those who it is well known believe that the Church of do-day, or rather its members, are failing in their duty to their fellow men. Now with regard to the meeting.lt was clear that most of those present considered and gave expression to their feeling that our present social system was radically wrong—in common language—that most people found the conditions of life hard, not because they would not work or that the beneficent Creator had not supplied the world with a bountiful and luscious supply of that which was necessary for life, but that owing to our social system only a few were able t 6 get hold and enjoy these fruits of the earth. It was also mentioned in detail that most of the intemperance and vicious living caused by poverty, and consequent overcrowding, arose out of our present Social System. Very naturally many remedies were suggested, and some of the speakers were bold enough to declare that no help or very little could arise from either the Conservative or Liberal parties. From the Conserva- tive party it was felt "there was absolutely no hope. From the Liberal party it was stated there might be a little but most of the speakers appeared to consider that most help would come through the Labour party. Of course the whole solution ot the Social Problem will have.to come from the people themselves. God helps those people and nations who use his gifts aright, and therefore there must be wise governnents. No one political organisation can do it. Political parties are only machinery and aids after all. The best thing is for the people to use the machinery of I these parties for the particular good of the community at large. Now what is going to be done in Warrington? It was clear last Sunday evening that the loaders of the working classes are in earnest ovEr their intentions to improve the conditions if they can, of those they represent, and that they will, if .possible, bring out a Labour candidate as a preliminary step. On the other hand the Liberal party's programme is one that will make for the advancement and betterment of the people. However, it would be a foolish thing for both the Labour and Liberal parties to bring out a candidate. What I would suggest is that a con- ference should be arranged between the two parties and an understanding arrived at, so that there should

be only one candidate from both parties. In fact they should agree to help each other. If not there will be a political tragedy in Warrington and once more the enemies of the labouring classes will obtain the Parliamentary seat. The meeting chose the following men as a committee to consider Mr. Dunnico's project :—Messrs. Morton, Osborne, England, Trow, Nightingale, Edwardson, Elwell, Lloyd Jones, Reeves,Councillor Stott and two others whose names I cannot for the moment remem- ber. All these men are well known either as Labour men, or as men who are students of Socialogy, and their joint deliberation should be of some value.

GREAT SANKEY'S AFFAIRS. Meeting of the Parish Council,

A meeting of the Great Sankey Parish Council was held in the County Council schools on Monday evening. Mr. G. Thompson presided and there were also present: Messrs. !G. Higgs. William Furness, W. Taylor, H. Fairclough, with J. Smith (clerk).

===== 0002601_19040604_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
BROW FARM WATER LUPPLY.

The Clerk read a letter from Mr. Deas, the water engineer, stating that the Corporation would run a main to a point opposite Brow Farm, if the Parish Council would guarantee 8 per cent on the cost of such main for a term of ten years,at an estimated cost of £75. —The clerk said he wrote for a further explanation with regard to the guarantee, inquiring whether the 8 per cent. on the cost was to be in addition to the amount paid for water. Mr. Deas wrote in reply :—" With further reference to thesupply of water to Brow Farm and property adjoining, we will extend the main to opposite the above-mentioned property pro- vided your Council will guarantee the sum of 26 per annum for a term of ten years. The guarantee is only paid should the water rates received be below the guaranteed amount. If the water rates received amounted to £4 per annum then the Parish Council would be responsible to the Corporation for £2, making a total of £6 per annum. Mr. Furness: Have you had any other application in ieference to the supply of water? The Chairman: We expect that some others will take the water, and that ultimately the whole expense will be covered by the amount received from the water rates of consumers. The committee visited the farm along with Mr. Smethurst in order to assure themselves that there would be no difficulty in running water into the farm from the main road. Mr. Smethurst agreed to do his share of the work if the main were run to the gate. Mr. Taylor, the occupier of the farm, was very much in want of water. The committee tried the pump and found very little water there. It was a serious matter to Mr. Taylor to be without an adequate supply. Mr. Furness said it occurred to him that this was more a question to be settled between the landlord and the Water Committee. It would not be quite fair to the parishioners to establish the precedent of guarantee- ing such an expenditure in the interests of one individual. It was unfair to pledge the rates for an isolated case. If Mr. Taylor, the tenant, brought pressure to bear on the landlord, the landlord would himself take the responsibility. The landlord was responsible to see that the tenant had his requirements for water attended to. The Chairman: We are responsible. Mr. Furness said he did not question the statement but it seemed to him an absurd position for the Council to be called upon to pledge the rates in the interests of one individual. He understood that a member of the Parish Council objected to pledge the parochial rate for a small section of the community some years ago with the result that the Water Committee took Vas responsibility themselves. The Chairman: They had a guarantee from certain people. Mr. Furness: That should be done in this case. By bringing pressure upon the owners, Mr. Taylor could make them take the responsibility. , The Chairman: It would be impossible to make them take it.

Mr. Higgs said he presumed the responsibility of the Parish Council only attachel to the cost of laying the main. The landlord was responsible for any other cost The Chairman said the committee had gone fully into the matter, and in the interests of the public they were desirous of getting the entire cost covered, but up to the present they had not succeeded. Mr. Higgs: What will be the proportion of cost to us?

_ The Clerk said Mr. Taylor's requirements would work out at £4 155., • leaving £1 53. to be provided by the parish. Mr. Taylor said he thought Mr. Goodier would take the water when the mains were laid. He (Mr. Taylor). had endeavoured to get along with the water from the well on the farm but the supply was now very small, and Mr. Kerfoot, the inspector to the Rural Council, had threatened to report him if he did not take steps to improve the water supply. Mr. Furness: This is done quite as much in the interests of the landlord as in your own interests. If there is no water supply, the property depreciates in value.

The Chairman said there was no doubt that the Rural District Council would grant the request to provide the water supply because the Inspector had condemned the present supply. The township would have to meet the expense, whatever was said at that meeting. No time was to be lost as the District Council met on Wed- nesday and would take the necessary steps to carry out the scheme.

Mr. Furness moved that the matter be referred back to the District Council. There was no seconder.

The Clerk said that if the Rural Council sanctioned the scheme there was not the slightest doubt that the cost would be a charge on the rates of Great Sankey. Mr. Farness contended that owners of property were compelled to see that there was a supply of water. After further discussion, Mr. Furness moved that the following resolution be sent to the Rural District Coun- cil : "That the members of this Council consider that Mr. Taylor should have an adequate supply of water." Mr. Higgs seconded and the resolution was carried. The Chairman said the committee felt very strongly upon the same lines that Mr. Furness had been arguing upon. The committee had been very jealous for the Council's interests.

THE SANITARY COLLECTION. The Clerk farnishecfa reteern on the cost of sanitary collection. The Council decided to consider the matter in committee.

THE ILLNESS OF MR. HIGGS. The Chairman said he was sure they were all pleased to see Mr. Higgs amongstthem once more. They hoped his recovery was permanent, and that he would be able to devote his time and energy to the interests of the township. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Higgs acknowledged the good wishes of the mem- bers, and aid they might really rely upon him doing his very best.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DELIVERY OF LETTERS.

The Clerk stated that in accordance with the Instruc• tions of the Council, he wrote to the Warrington Post- master calling his attention to the inconvenience caused to residents by letters addressed to Great Sankey being sent on to Penketh in the first instance and then returned, causing much delay from time to time. In reply, Mr. Lister, the postmaster. said that the correct postal address was Sankey Bridges, not Great Sankey. He had, however, given instructions to the staff, so far as might be possible, to put all correspond- ence in the proper channel whether addressed correctly or to Great Sankey. He hoped there would be no further cause for complaint. The Chairman contended that the correct address was Great Sankey, although the postal authorities chose for their own convenience to call it Sankey Bridges. There was no doubt that the postal authorities were dealing with the complaint and endeavouring to remove it. He had had an instance of it during the past week. _

The clerk said that when the letters were forwarded to Penketh in mistake they were received at 4 o'clock in the afternoon, instead of at 7 o'clock in the morning.

THE LAMPLIGHTER'S WAGES.

Tim clerk stated that he wrote to the secretary of the Gas Committee, complaining of one of the lamps being broken, and suggesting that the wages of the lamplighter might be left at.the clerk's house so that he could call for them.

Mr. Taylor wrote in reply that the custom had been for the lamplighter to meet the foreman every Monday morning. In future the arrangement suggested by the Council would be carried out.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
REDUCTION IN THE PRICE OF GAS.

The Town Clerk wrote in reply to a communication from the clerk that the Gas Committee had decided to increase the disconnt to consumers of gas outside the Borough by threepence per thousand • cubic feet as and from the 31st March last.

The Clerk explained that the discount allowed on gas bills to consumers outside the Borough would in future be ninepence per thousand feet instead of sixpence. The Chairman said the lower the price the more would people be inclined to become consumers of gas. Mr. Higgs said most of the people in Hood-lane now used gas. The Chairman thought they miglit hope for a reduc- tion in the price of lamps for public lighting. The Clerk said the price now stood at 22135. 9d. per lamp.

===== 0002601_19040604_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
TEE REMOVAL OF DEAD BODIES.

The Chairman called attention to the fact that a dead body recovered from the Sankey Canal was allowed to remain at the Chapel House mortuary for four days. The body had bean in the water some eight or ten days, and the members would have some idea of the nuisance and inconvenience caused to Nit% Unsworth in having a body so long on his premises. He thought, perhaps, a recommandatton could be made to prevent anything of the kind occurrin., in future. Mr. Furness asked whether the fault lay with the Coroner.

The Clerk said the fault lay between the Coroner and the police. The Chairman said the authorities could not get the body identified. _ _ _ •

The Clerk thought the difficulty might be obviated if a burial certificate were given by the Coroner at the earliest possible moment. It was decided to leave the matter over for the present. 'This was all the business of public interest.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Should the Boys Wear Corduroy?

The members of the Warrington Board of Guardians met on Wednesday morning. Mr. A. Brooks presided and there were also present: Mrs. Wilkinson, Rev. Father Mercer,Rev. T. R. Matthews, Dr. Patterson, Dr. Murray, Messrs. J. W. Knowles, S. Davies, A. J. Jolley, G. Thompson, T. H. Ashcroft, J. Clarke, J. Payton, G. Mather, T. Ritchie, R. Forshaw, W. Flannagan, J. Turton, E. L. Bent, and J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright (superintendent Padgate schools).

DAY AND NIGHT CHARGE NURSES.

Nurse Bray having resigned the position of night charge nurse at the Workhouse hospital, Nurse Simmonds was promoted to take her place. Nurse Caunt of Nottingham, was appointed day charge nurse. She was the only applicant for the position. Nurse Bray has gone to Aldershot.

CORDUROY V. TWEED. THE CLOTHING OF PADGATE SCHOOL BOYS.

The Padgate Schools Committee reported that the Superintendent submitted some information about the use of tweed instead of curduroy for the use of the boys. After considerable discussion, it was resolved that tweed be used instead of corduroy. It was also recom- mended that the tender of Messrs. E. Forshaw and Sons of Warrington, for a new boiler at the schools at a cost of £B5 be accepted. Mr. MATHER in moving the confirmation of the minutes, said the committee were forced by the inspector of boilers to purchase a new boiler, as the existing one was worn out. The resolution with regard to the clothing of the boys spoke for itself. He had gone into the question of the relative cost of tweed and corduroy. There was a general impression that the cost of tweed would be greater, but from statistics and other information he had obtained, he found that the expense would be little, if any, morel than the present cost for corduroy. The average cost per lad per annum for corduroy was is. The cost of tweed, sufficient for a suit, would be about 4s. 3d. per boy. The cost might be a little more for tweed .than was at present paid for corduroy, but be did not think the Guardians would object. As Guardians they were not elected solely to relieve destitution but to re- duce pauperism, if possible. (Hear,hear.) The schools at Padgate were built with the object of removing the children from the influence of the Workhouse and pauperism. The inmates of the Workhouse were dressed in corduroy and under the existing arrangement, the boys at the schools were dressed in exactly the same material, and were pointed out as being young paupers. He for one would like to see the corduroy changed for tweed. Another reason for the change was that the smell of corduroy was very disagreeable. When they got 100 boys together in one room and all wearing corduroy, the smell was unbearable. A good many of the Padgate School boys attended the same church as himself (Mr. Mather) and he could tell them that the smell of corduroy was very disagreeable. The clothing of the girls at Padgate had been changed for some time and he did not know why the boys had been left to wear corduroy unless it was that the members preferred that the boys should wear a uniform. At Bolton the boys wore corduroy whilst in the schools, but were allowed to wear a tweed suit when outside. The increased cost of the proposed change would be very slight for a num- rber of years, as the boys had corduroy suits already,and I would only wear tweed when going outside the institution. _ _ _

Mrs.WILEINEoN seconded the adoption of the minutes. Mr. E. L. BENT supported and thought the change would be a great blessing to the boys. It was rather objectionable to see the boys wearing suits of the same material as the adult paupers. Mr. DAVIES said he liked to be loyal to his com- mittee, but as the mover of the change he wished to say that he was somewhat misled as to the cost. He did not say he was deliberately misled, but he was led to believe that the cost would be about the same or less. Since then, he had ascertained that the change would be an extra charge of from £lOO to £1.50 per annum. If that was so he could not bind himself to support an expensive change of that kind. Mr. J. W. KNOWLES thought the Board ought to keep in view the advisability of not making the standard of the workhouse boy's condition better than that of the boy of a labouring man, who by thrifty means, did his best for his family. Could a labouring man with wages of 225. a week afford to buy tweed suits for his lads? As Guardians they representpd the ratepayers, and the cottager had to pay his share of the rates in his rent. Was it fair to the thrifty work- ingman that he should see these Workhouse boys better clothed than he could afford to clothe his own at home? One would soon expect to hear the remark that it was better to be a pauper than a workingman, and that was what would happeng some people could have their way. If the smell of corduroy was offensive, one had to put up with little matters of thift kind, even in church. The boys looked neat and clean in curduroy. Mr. MATHER : Question.

The CLERK said there was evidently a misapprehen- sion as to the difference in cost. The difference was practically very small. Mr. KNOWLES : You must also reckon the wearing quality of the two materials as well "as the cost. Mr. Knowles concluded by moving an amendment that the minute referring to the adoption of tweed be referred back.

Mr. THOMPSON seconded the amendment. It was rather a serious matter, he said, if the cost was to be much greater. He understood from Mr. Mather that it would make very little difference. The matter seemed to have been sprung upon the Board rather hurriedly. Mr. Bent : It has been well fought. out in com- mittee.

Rev. FA.TIIER MERCER said he had consulted a prac- tical man on the question of cost and he considered that the initial cost of the suits would be greater. When they took into consideration the question of wear and tear, the change from corduroy to tweed would cost half as much again. At the committee meeting, the question was considered of allowing the boys a tweed- suit each for Sunday wear and special occasions. He would suggest that the matter be considered from that point of view by the Board. Mr. A. J. JOLLEY supported the amendment, and ob- served that he did not always find himself in sympathy with Mr. Knowles. He would like to emphasise the principles Mr. Knowles laid down. They seemed to him to be unanswerable and they ought to prompt all their actions. Mr. Mather endeavoured to show that the Board was going to save money and then he after- wards argued strictly from the point of view of senti- ment. The argument of economy could be defeated in a very few moments as it was not sound. The posi- tion of Dr. Mercer on the point was clear and vary strong on the point.lt was not onlya question of initial cost but of wear and tear. The question was very carefully gone into some years ago and the experiment was tried, but the expense was found to be considerably greater than anticipated and accordingly it was decided to revert to the corduroy uniform. It was the duty of the guardians whilst adhering to the general principles governing in. relief and out-relief, not to make the lot of a Pauper, whether an adult or a child, one to be coveted or desired. But on the other hand they ought not to do anything which would tend to imbue the idea of pauperism into the minds of children. They ought to remove it as far as possible. If the proposed change would help forward that he should strongly support it, but he did not see that it was likely to achieve that end. Something had been said:about the stigma of pauperism and the smell of corduroy. He had heard of the odour of sanctity but he did not know whether that had anything to do with corduroy. (Laughter.) Corduroys were used amongst certain classee, and particularly in this part of the country. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Mather referred to Bolton, but there the children were clothed in corduroy, except on Sundays, and the children were housed in a work- house. Bolton was" miles behind "Warrington in re- gard to the treatment of pauper children and the Bolton authority endeavoured to slightly ameliorate the ccsndition of the children by providing them with plain clothes when they were outside the institution. The suggestion had been made that he (Mr. Jolley) had been an advocate of extravagance—a suggestion he strongly repudiated—as he had always endeavoured in the best sense of the word to stick up for ecoamny. It was on the ground of economy that he supported the amendment. Mr. AsHcaopr said he had been visiting Padgate for a number of years, but he had never heard a word of complaint as to the use of corduroy. From his own experience he knew that tweed would cost more. Rev. T. R. MA.Trincws did not think the subject would suffer by a little delay. Mr. FORSHAW said when a boy went out of the schools or proceeded home he was known as a Work- house boy by the corduroy he wore. He thought it would be better if snob a distinction were removed.

-.-...- Mr. BILLINGE pointed out that the cost of out-relief in Warrington had been increased by .£650 during the year, and the Newton district had also increased their grants by £5OO. .This was not a time to speud money on innovations. There were many working people who were honestly trying to bring up their families on less than 22.5. a week, and it would be a mistake to popular- ise their poor law schools by taking the step indicated. Mr. CLA.RKE supported the amendment. He was not entirely opposed to the change but the matter might wait a little. Mr. DANIES hoped Mr. Mather would not accuse him of disloyalty. Mr. MATHER : Tt was just the very thing I wasgoing to do.(Laughter). Mr. Davies was the person to propose the resolution in committee and now he came to the Board and spoke against it. He (Mr. Mather) thought the discussion bad been the best he had heard in the Board room, and had certainly brought out the oratorical powers of the members. He still contended that the cost of tweed in place of corduroy would be very little more. The present cost of corduroy was £37 7s. 6d. per annum. He did not know from where Mr. Davies had got his figures. The cost per boy was is. Tweed would cost about 4s. 3d. per suit, and as the suits were made oa the premises, the cost would not be very great. Economy with regard to poor-law administration was one of his (Mr. Mather's) strong points, but he believed it was false economy to con tinue with corduroy: He had made inquiries from cottage homes in different places, and the bulk of the answers were in favour of tweed. On a vote being taken the amendment was carried and the minute was referred back.

A WORN-OUT BOILER.

Mr. BILLINGE asked whether it was a fact that the inspector of boilers had condemn3cl the use of the boiler at the schools.

Mr. MATHER said the inspector not only condemned the boiler, but gave the committee three months in which to replace it. The CHAIRMAN said the letter accompanying the renort hardly bore out that statement. The lettersaid : "Our inspector has made a thorough examination of your boiler and encloses his report. You will see from it that a new furnace should be provided within the next three months."

Mr. THOMPSON said there was nothing in the report to warrant the Board in buying a new boiler. He moved that the matter be referred back. Mr. KNOWLES seconded.

Mr. A. J. JOLLEY' said The boiler had been in a dan- gerous condition for some time. As a matter of fact the boiler had only been allowed to work at 25 lbs pressure. The committee were not entirely destitute of business ability, and in this matter they had acted in the most straightforward and businesslike way. They got two first class firms to advise them in the matter. It was found that the wisest course to take was to ask for tenders for a now boiler and the committee now recommended the acceptance of a tender for £B5. They were not over burdened with officials at Pa,dEtate, and the firing up was sometimes done by boys. The boiler, which was bought second-hand, was worn out, and the gauge-glass was not trustworthy. If the Board got this new boiler they would have no fear of any risk to life and property. and it would be more '- economical because they could work at higher pressure. He hoped the amendment would not be carried.

Mr. NIITIIER said the inspector gave them three months as the working life of the boiler. When the bridge came to be removed out of the flue water oozed out. Inquiries were made as to putting in a new flue, and it was ascertained that a new flue would cost as much as a new boiler, and they would still have aa old boiler.

Mr. FORSHA.W real eKtra3ts from th 3 inspactor's report and said it was clear that the boiler was worn out.

_ Mr. TURTON said on one occasion, a new boiler was asked for, when all that w is wanted was a patch on the old boiler. He felt satisfied in this case tk at a new one was required. . .

The amendment was withdrawn and the minutes were passed.

CONGESTED STATE OF TH.E HOUSE

At a meeting of the House Committee, held on May 6th, the sub-committee appointed to dal. with the con- gested state of the house were unanimously authorised in conjunction with the Master, to visit the Bolton Union and to inspect the methods employed there in dealing with able-bodied poor; also to visit the Tarvin Union Chester, which was reported to be nearly empty. Both these visits will open up possibilities for dealing with the question of overcrowding and better enable the sub-committee to frame their report and make sug- gestions to the Board for meeting the difficulties.—At meeting held on May 13th, Mr. Fors'mv-directed atten- tion to the entrance of the Workhouse and the very objectionable features in connection with it. It was resolved to fully discuss at the next meeting the advisa- bility of providing a different entrance. At a subsequent meeting. Mr. Forshaw drew attention to the entrance to the Workhouse and proposed that the Guardians take steps to purchase the' two houses adjoining the lodge. This would open up space and also give an entrance from Wakefield-street, The matter, however, dropped. At a further meeting on May 27, it wai resolved that the Master obtain competitive tenders for the supply and fittinc, up of a beef tea extractor. It was resolved to place the Chaplain of the Workhouse, the Rev. W. Brace- eamp, upon the same terms as the Board's medical officers, namely. that the Guardians pay the telephone rate, as the chaplain's telephone is chiefly used for Workhouse purposes. . _

The Comniittee also recommended the acceptance of the,tender of Mr. Whittaker for good fresh fish at 3id.per lb., and continuance of appointment of Mrs. Carrington in the laundry as required at 3s. per day. Mr. 4. J. JOLLEY moved the confirmation of the minutes. Referring to the congested state of the House, he said the sub-Committee had prepared their report, and it would be considered at a special meeting of the committee to be held on Friday. The whole matter would thenmome before the Board. At the last meeting of the sub-committee a suggestion was thrown out that it would be desirable to have the advice and presence of Mr. Jenner Fast, the Local Government Biard Impector, whilst considering the matter. lie (Mr. Jolley) wrote to Mr. Jenner Fust,and had received a reply stating that he would be present. Mr. FORSHAW seconded the adoption of the minutes. _ . . .

Mr. THOMPSON called attention to the fish contract, and said the Board ought to issue this contract with the other half-yearly contracts, and give every fishmonger In the town a chance of tendering. Mr. MATHER said it was exactly what the committee intended to do. Hitherto there had been no contracts issued for fish, and this contract had been accepted until the half-year's end, when it would be advertised for in the usual way. Instead of paying Bd. a paund for fish. the Board was now getting it at 31d. Mr. BILLINGE thought the report of the sub- committee ought to be placed in the hands of the mem- bers before it was oonsidered in the presence of Mr. Jenner Fast. The Guardians did not intend to go in for a big expenditure of money, but he was afraid that wasta course Mr. Jenner Fust would advocate. It was decided that the retort of the sub-committee should be printed and circulated amongst the moralisers of the House Committee.

SCARLET FEVER.

The Health Committee has received a letter from the Local Government Board alluding to the returns for the last quarter, which included 15 deaths from scarlet fever in Warrington, and asking for a report on the recent prevalence of the disease.—The Medical Officer has been instructed to prepare,and forward such a report.

IVIARGARINE7MAK_ING AT _BANK QUAY.

Thia Health Committee has resolved,that the premises of Messrs. J. Crosfleld and Sons Ltd., Bank Quay be registered for the manufacture of margarine under the Margarine Act, 1887.

TfIE BAND STAND.

The Town Hall and Parks Committee has, in response to a request from the Bands Association,decided to pro- vide a set of iron music stands. The stand is also to be thoroughly cleaned. The cost of wind-guards was found to be £l5, and the committee decided not to pro- ceed with this work.

COUNCILLOR STOTT AND A WAGE QUESTION.

At a Water Committee meeting on May 9th a letter was read from Mr. Councillor Stott, stating that in his opinion the wages paid to the man recently appointed to assist the stoker at the Winwick Pumping Station are insufficient, and asking, the committee to consider the matter. Resolved, that the Town Clerk be instructed to inform Mr. Stott that, in the opinion of the com- mittee. the wages paid are ample for the services rendered. ELECTRICITY AT STOCKTON HEATH. "

A letter was read from the clerk to the Stockton Heath parish Council consenting to the Corporation supplying their parish with electricity on condition that the prices charged for electricity and gas be not in excess of the charges within the borough. ORFORD LANE TRAMWAY EXTENSION. The Tramways Manager reports as follows oh the above: The 'cost of a line from Town's End to the King and Queen.part of which would have to be double track owing to the narrowness of the road, would cost £7,400. Taking the number of people passing up and down this road at even figures, that is 60,000, to make it pay or just hold its own, 22 per cent, would have to be I passengers, or a riding population of 13,200 per week, which would give receipts at £55 at 9.4 d per car mile. Against this would come two cars running 1,400 miles a week at a total working cost of 9•ld. per car mile.which is £55 (9.4 d. was the total working expenses last year.) do not think we should get much traffic from this side of Sharp-street until such time as the cars are able to run into the centre of the town, as the distance is somewhat short; we should certainly not get 22 per cent. Taking the top end of the road, Marsh House-lane and around the King and Queen district, from what I have myself carefully noted, most of the

people at present use the Cemetery cars; this, of course, ! they would not do had they cars passing their houses. I think that if a limo was run up Otford-lane, and even if it pays its way, which I greatly doubt, it would tap the Cemetery section to such an extent that from being a paying route it would show a deficit. The number of 'bus passengers, 182 per day, does not speak very favour- ably for the success of the car system, as I am sure if there were a good riding population these people would be the first to take advantage of it. Taking everything into consideration, I do not think it would be advisable to construct a line along Orford-lane, as I feel sure it would be run at a loss and also cause a loss on the Cemetery Section.—J. TELFER, Tramways Manager.

SPECIAL MOTIONS. 1. By Mr. Councillor Stott.—That in the opinion of this Council the wages paid to the stoker appointed for the Winwick Pumping Station in March last are not sufficient for the number of hours he is employed per week, and that the Water Committee should immediately consider the question of an increase of wages being given._

-2. By Mr. Councillor Ellison.—That the Paving and Sewerage Committee be requested to carry out the resolution passed by this Council on July 7th, 1903, as follows :—Resolved, that the footpath on the north side of Knutsford-road from corner of Thelwal-llane to the railway bridge be formed and flagged. Also that the Paving and Sewerage Committee be requested to make adequate arrangements for the watering of the streets and roads in the upper portion of Latchford Ward :—Knntsford-road, from Black Bear Bridge to Railway Bridge, Wash-lane, Lower Wash, Grappenhall- road to Railway Bridge, Thelwall-lane to Ashfleld House.

3. By Councillor Maginn.—That it be an instruction to each committee of the Corporation when they have obtained tenders for goods or for labour required, or both, to accept subject to Standing Order 47 (2) the lowest tender, except only in cases where any person tendering carries on business within the Borough, and in which the committee for special reasons may con- sider it advisable not to accept the lowest tender, and that in accepting any tender each 'committee may require the persons tendering to give the names of two respon- sible sureties who shall guarantee, or may require a deposit to secure, the due execution of the contract. That a complete list of all tenders shall be entered in a book to be kept for that purpose, and to be open to inspection of members of the Council.

PRESENTATION TO FATHER RYAN. His Work at the Chveil of the Sacred Heart.

The respect and esteem in which the Rev. Father Ryan is held by the congregation at the Church of the Sacred Heart, Bank Quay, amongst whom he has laboured for the past twelve years, found cordial expression on Monday evening. To celebrate the silver jubilee of the rev, father's ordination to the priesthood, a happy gathering was held in the schoolroom, and Father Ryan was the recipient of a purse containing £33 in gold, and an illuminated address, subscribed to by the congrega- tion. There was a large attendance, Dr. Liston presid- ing. There were also present on the platform : Mr. David Houghton, Mr. P. J. George (secretary of the testimonial fund), Mr. J. Gibbins (treasurer), and Mr. G. Coggins. Among the audience were the Revs. Father S. Clarke (Widnes), O'Neill (Tyldesley), Rimmer (Leigh), Bernard and Joseph (Phantasaph), McGrath and D'Arcy (Latchford).

The CHAIRMAN made the presentation, and read the address as follows :—" To the Rev. Michael Ryan, P.P. Church of the Sacred Heart, Warrington, Dear Rev Father,—We, the members of your congregation respect- fully offer our hearty congratulations upon the silver jubilee of your ordination to the priesthood, and ask yout acceptance of a purse of gold as a token of our respect, esteem, and goodwill, with the very best wishes that you may have good health to continue in the ser- vice of the Church for many years to come. We are filled with admiration of, and gratitude for, your labours amongst us for the past 12 years. We recall the early days, when the mission was opened in a very humble way, and the small congregation which then gathered round you were glad to second your efforts for the complete establishment of the Sacred Heart Mission. In proportion to the increase of the congregation we saw our place of worship grow from a small room step by step until finally we had our admirable church erected, of which priest and parishioners can justly feel proud. We are aware of your desire to complete with fitting decoration the interior of the church, especially the sanctuary. We pray and hope that you may receive every support in the accomplishment of your noble purpose. In making you this offering, rev, father, we feel sure it will not be regarded-for its monetary value alone, but that it will be enhanced by the knowledge that it has emanated from your flock, who in a hearty and affec- tionate manner are desirous of co-operating and contri- buting something which they, the members of your flock, hope will be accepted as an expression of their love and goodwill towards you as their pastor.—Signed on behalf of the congregation, James Dore Liston (president), George Coggins (vice-president), John Gibbins (treasurer), P. J. George (secretary), Joseph Still* Charles Mounfield, John Marsh, David Houghton, Joseph Peers. May 30, 1901." In presenting the purse a id address, the Chairman asked Father Ryan to accept the gifts as a token of sincere esteem and goodwill. (Applause.) Mr. DAVID EIorIGHTON said that if there was one part of the address -nore than another to which he would call Father Ryan's attention, it was where the address spoke of a spontaneous desire on the part of the congrega- tion to contribute to the testimonial. He was pleased to see Father Joseph among the audience—(loud applause)—and he was sure the rev, father would admit that even during the grand mission he conducted he never brought them together in such grand numbers as were to be seen that evening. All had joined most heartily in th e testimonial, and no collector had met with a single r 9fusal. (Hear, hear, and applause.) 'Bea Rev. Father RYA,N who was very cordiall received, said he considered he had done nothiag but his ordinary duty. Most of those present knew what a small beginning they had, but the congrega- tion assisted him in every way they could. He came to Warrington about 12 years ago as anabso- lute stranger. He did not say this in a disparaging manner, but there were some who were not so free with him, ani they had to inquire whether they should assist him or not. After a short time, however, that state of things was completely changed,. and the mission pro- gressed most favourably. He did not think in such a comparatively short time there had been such a coni- pleteestablishment in the whole of the diocese, (Hear, hear.) Some 20 or 30 years were generally required to establish a mission, and sometimes more to establish a house, church and schools. When he came to Warring- ton they had none of these things, but after the short period of six years they became firmly established, land were comparatively little in debt. His jubilee as a priest passed some time ago—(laughter) —and he thought that was one reason for the presenta- tion, But he thought there was another reason, and that was that if he had not gone to the Holy Land recently, probably the presentation might not have been got up. (Renewed laughter.) His journey to the Holy Land was most interesting, his fellow traveller being Father O'Neill. The journeys they undertook were long, but when they as Catholics thought of what they had seen and the places they had visited, it gave them courage and strength to de far more for the glory of God than they had been able to do up to the present. (Applause.) The rev, gentleman, in conclusion, said he admired the presentation, not for its monetary value, but for the loving hearts which prompted it. (Applause.) Father O'NEILL thanked the audience for having per- mitted him to be present that night to testify his grati- tude to, and his appreciation of, his fellow pilgrim to the Holy Land, Father Ryan. During the evening an eajoyable musical programme was submitted. Songs were contributed in very credit- able style by Mrs. J. D. Liston, Miss E. Davies, Miss A. Fairclough,Messrs. G. Nel,vton,P.lJ.George,J. Gibbins, G. Kendrick, and G. Coggins. Mr. W. H. Kendrick discharged the duties of accompanist with ability. A uccessful eith elected )," wina the sin;iag of F aith of our Fathers."

Baptismal Service at Hill Cliffe.

Hill Cliffe Baptist Chapel was again crowded to over, flowing on Sunday evening last on the occasion gi another Blptismal service. The pastor, the Rev. J. Hughes preached a special sermon on the place to' importance of baptism in the economy of Christi-t Truth, dwelling on the importance of baptism, which seen in the place assigned to it in the Bible record Will,'" more frequently referred to and enjoined, than axle" other ordinance and most other doctrines in the Ner4 Testament. This is further seen in the place Jesus Chrn gave to it in his life and teaching, inaugurating " ministry by submitting to it and closing his earthLf mission by enjoining it. He also treated of the Pl.3,ce' given to it in the ministry of the Apostles and tuef individual life of believers, closing with the relation ° Baptism to the Church and showing how dvarture frog]. the original method of baptism h ,aed the door for differea errors, rites and ritaatiitic practices,, tho' ethical purpose of baptism being largely if not enttrell lost sight of. After the sermon, four parsons weg• immersed, and great and reverent interest was taken "' the large congregation both in the sermon and the baptism, and other baptisms are likely to follow.
